FNETS: Factor-adjusted network estimation and forecasting for high-dimensional time series
Matteo Barigozzi, Haeran Cho, Dom Owens

Abstract
We propose FNETS, a methodology for network estimation and forecasting of high-dimensional time series exhibiting strong serial- and cross-sectional correlations. We operate under a factor-adjusted vector autoregressive (VAR) model which, after accounting for pervasive co-movements of the variables by common factors, models the remaining idiosyncratic dynamic dependence between the variables as a sparse VAR process. Network estimation of FNETS consists of three steps: (i) factor-adjustment via dynamic principal component analysis, (ii) estimation of the latent VAR process via ℓ 1 \ell_{1} -regularised Yule-Walker estimator, and (iii) estimation of partial correlation and long-run partial correlation matrices. In doing so, we learn three networks underpinning the VAR process, namely a directed network representing the Granger causal linkages between the variables, an undirected one embedding their contemporaneous relationships and finally, an undirected network that summarises both lead-lag and contemporaneous linkages. In addition, FNETS provides a suite of methods for forecasting the factor-driven and the idiosyncratic VAR processes. Under general conditions permitting tails heavier than the Gaussian one, we derive uniform consistency rates for the estimators in both network estimation and forecasting, which hold as the dimension of the panel and the sample size diverge. Simulation studies and real data application confirm the good performance of FNETS.

Keywords:
vector autoregression, network estimation, forecasting, dynamic factor modelling.

1
Introduction

Vector autoregressive (VAR) models are popularly adopted for time series analysis in economics and finance.
Fitting a VAR model to the data enables inferring dynamic interdependence between the variables as well as forecasting the future.
VAR models are particularly appealing for network analysis
since estimating the non-zero elements of the VAR parameter matrices, a.k.a. transition matrices,
recovers directed edges between the components of vector time series
in a Granger causality network.
In addition, by estimating a precision matrix
(inverse of the covariance matrix) of the VAR innovations,
we can also define a network capturing contemporaneous linear dependencies.
For the network interpretation of VAR modelling, see e.g.
Dahlhaus, 2000
;
Eichler, 2007
;
Billio et al., 2012
;
Ahelegbey et al., 2016
;
Barigozzi and Brownlees, 2019
;
Guðmundsson and Brownlees, 2021
;
Uematsu and Yamagata, 2023a
.

Estimation of VAR models quickly becomes a high-dimensional problem
as the number of parameters grows quadratically with the dimensionality.
There is a mature literature on estimation of high-dimensional VAR models under the sparsity

(
Hsu et al., 2008
;
Song and Bickel, 2011
;
Basu and Michailidis, 2015
;
Han et al., 2015
;
Kock and Callot, 2015
;
Barigozzi and Brownlees, 2019
;
Nicholson et al., 2020
)
and low-rank plus sparsity
(
Basu et al., 2019
)
assumptions,
see also
De Mol et al., 2008
and
Bańbura et al., 2010
for Bayesian approaches.
In all above, either explicitly or implicitly, the spectral density of the time series is required to have eigenvalues which are uniformly bounded over frequencies.
Indeed, this condition is crucial for controlling the deviation bounds involved in theoretical investigation of regularised estimators.

Figure 1
:
Left: The two largest eigenvalues (
y
-axis) of the long-run covariance matrix
estimated from the volatility panel analysed in Section
5.3

(March
2008
to March
2009
,
n=252
)
with subsets of cross-sections randomly sampled
100
times
for each given dimension
p\in\{5,\ldots,46\}
(
x
-axis).
Right: logged and truncated
p
-values
(truncation level chosen by Bonferroni correction with the significance level
0.1
)
from fitting a VAR(
5
) model to the same dataset using ridge regression and generating
p
-values corresponding to each coefficient as described in
Cule et al., 2011
.
For each pair of variables (corresponding tickers given in
x
- and
y
-axes), the minimum
p
-value over the five lags is reported.

Lin and Michailidis, 2020
observe that for VAR processes, this assumption restricts the parameters to be either dense but small in their magnitude (which makes their estimation using the shrinkage-based methods challenging) or highly sparse,
while
Giannone et al., 2021
note the difficulty of identifying sparse predictive representations in many economic applications.
Moreover, some datasets typically exhibit strong serial and cross-sectional correlations and violate the bounded spectrum assumption.
The left panel of Figure
1
provides an illustration of this phenomenon;
with the increase of dimensionality, a volatility panel dataset (see Section
5.3
for its description) exhibits a linear increase in the leading eigenvalue of the estimate of its spectral density matrix at frequency
0
(i.e. long-run covariance).
The right panel visualises the outcome from fitting a VAR(
5
) model to the same dataset
without making any adjustment of the strong correlations (see the caption for further details),
from which we cannot infer meaningful, sparse pairwise relationship.

In this paper, we propose to model high-dimensional time series by means of a factor-adjusted VAR approach, which simultaneously accounts for strong serial and cross-sectional correlations attributed to factors, as well as sparse, idiosyncratic correlations among the variables that remain after factor adjustment.
We take the most general approach to factor modelling based on the generalised dynamic factor model, where factors are dynamic in the sense that they are allowed to have not only contemporaneous but also lagged effects on the variables
(
Forni et al., 2000
)
.
We propose FNETS, a suite of tools accompanying the model for estimation and forecasting with a particular focus on network analysis, which addresses the challenges arising from the latency of the VAR process as well as high dimensionality.

We make the following methodological and theoretical contributions.

(a)

We propose an
\ell_{1}
-regularised Yule-Walker estimation method for estimating the factor-adjusted, idiosyncratic VAR, while permitting the number of non-zero parameters to slowly grow with the dimensionality.
Estimating the VAR parameters and the inverse of the innovation covariance, and then combining them allow us to define three networks underlying the latent VAR process, namely a direct network representing Granger causal linkages, an undirected one underpinning their contemporaneous relationships, as well as an undirected network summarising both.
Under general conditions permitting weak factors and heavier tails than the sub-Gaussian one, we show the consistency of FNETS in estimating the edge sets of these networks, which holds uniformly over all
p^{2}
entries of the networks (Propositions
3.3
and
3.5
).

(b)

We provide new consistency rates for the estimation and forecasting approaches considered by
Forni et al., 2005
;
Forni et al., 2017
, which hold uniformly for the entire cross-sections of
p
-dimensional time series (Propositions
4.1
and
B.2
).
In doing so, we establish uniform consistency of the estimators of high-dimensional spectral density matrices of the factor-driven and the idiosyncratic components, extending the results of
Zhang and Wu, 2021
to the presence of latent factors.

Our approach differs from the existing ones for factor-adjusted regression problems
(
Fan et al., 2020
;
Fan et al., 2021
;
Fan et al., 2023
;
Krampe and Margaritella, 2021
)
,
as: (i) it allows for the presence of dynamic factors, thus including all possible dynamic linear co-dependencies, and (ii) it relies only on the estimators of the autocovariances of the latent idiosyncratic process, and avoids estimating the entire latent process and controlling the errors arising from such a step, which increase with the sample size.
The price to pay for the generality of the factor modelling in (i), is an extra term appearing in the rate of consistency which represents the bandwidth for spectral density estimation required for factor-adjustment in the frequency domain.
We make explicit the role played by this bandwidth in the theoretical results, and also present the results under a more restricted static factor model for ease of comparison.
We mention two more differences between this paper and
Fan et al., 2021
;
Fan et al., 2023
.
First, they additionally consider the problem of testing hypotheses on the idiosyncratic covariance and the adequacy of factor/sparse regression, while we focus on network estimation.
Secondly, their methods accommodate models for the idiosyncratic component other than VAR.

FNETS is another take at the popular low-rank plus sparsity modelling framework
in the high-dimensional learning literature,
Also, it is in line with a frequently adopted practice in financial time series analysis
where factor-driven common components representing the systematic sources of risk,
are removed prior to inferring a network structure via (sparse) regression modelling
and identifying the most central nodes representing the systemic sources of risk
(
Diebold and Yılmaz, 2014
;
Barigozzi and Brownlees, 2019
)
.
We provide a rigorous theoretical treatment of this empirical approach
by accounting for the effect of the factor-adjustment step on the second step regression.

The rest of the paper is organised as follows.
Section
2
introduces the factor-adjusted VAR model.
Sections
3
and
4
describe
the network estimation and forecasting methodologies comprising FNETS, respectively,
and provide their theoretical consistency.
In Section
5
, we demonstrate the good estimation and forecasting performance of
FNETS on a panel of volatility measures.
Section
6
concludes the paper,
and all the proofs and complete simulation results are presented in Supplementary Appendix.
The R software
fnets
implementing FNETS is available from CRAN
(
Barigozzi et al., 2023
)
.

Notations.

By
\mathbf{I}
,
\mathbf{O}
and
\mathbf{0}
, we denote an identity matrix, a matrix of zeros and a vector of zeros whose dimensions depend on the context.
For a matrix
\mathbf{A}=[a_{ii^{\prime}},\,1\leq i\leq m,\,1\leq i^{\prime}\leq n]
,
we denote by
\mathbf{A}^{\top}
its transpose.
The element-wise
\ell_{\infty}
,
\ell_{0}
,
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
-norms are denoted
by
|\mathbf{A}|_{\infty}=\max_{1\leq i\leq m}\max_{1\leq i^{\prime}\leq n}|a_{ii^{\prime}}|
,

|\mathbf{A}|_{0}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n}\mathbb{I}_{\{a_{ii^{\prime}}\neq 0\}}
,

|\mathbf{A}|_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n}|a_{ii^{\prime}}|

and

|\mathbf{A}|_{2}=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n}|a_{ii^{\prime}}|^{2}}
.
The Frobenius, spectral, induced
L_{1}
and
L_{\infty}
-norms are denoted by

\|\mathbf{A}\|_{F}=|\mathbf{A}|_{2}
,

\|\mathbf{A}\|=\sqrt{\Lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})}

(with
\Lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{A})
and
\Lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{A})
denoting its largest and smallest eigenvalues in modulus),

\|\mathbf{A}\|_{1}=\max_{1\leq i^{\prime}\leq n}\sum_{i=1}^{m}|a_{ii^{\prime}}|

and
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}=\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{m}|a_{ii^{\prime}}|
.
Let
\mathbf{A}_{i\cdot}
and
\mathbf{A}_{\cdot k}
denote
the
i
-th row and the
k
-th column of
\mathbf{A}
.
For two real numbers, set
a\vee b=\max(a,b)
and
a\wedge b=\min(a,b)
.
Given two sequences
\{a_{n}\}
and
\{b_{n}\}
, we write
a_{n}=O(b_{n})

if, for some finite constant
C>0

there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}=\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
such that

|a_{n}||b_{n}|^{-1}\leq C
for all
n\geq N
; we denote by
O_{P}
the stochastic boundedness.
We write
a_{n}\asymp b_{n}
when
a_{n}=O(b_{n})
and
b_{n}=O(a_{n})
.
Throughout,
L
denotes the lag operator and
\iota=\sqrt{-1}
. Finally,
\mathbb{I}_{\mathcal{A}}=1
if the event
\mathcal{A}
takes place and
0
otherwise.

2
Factor-adjusted vector autoregressive model

Consider a zero-mean, second-order stationary
p
-variate process
\mathbf{X}_{t}=(X_{1t},\ldots,X_{pt})^{\top}
,
1\leq t\leq n
, which is decomposed into the sum of two latent components:
a factor-driven,
common
component
\bm{\chi}_{t}=(\chi_{1t},\ldots,\chi_{pt})^{\top}
,
and an
idiosyncratic
component
\bm{\xi}_{t}=(\xi_{1t},\ldots,\xi_{pt})^{\top}
modelled as a VAR process.
That is,
\mathbf{X}_{t}=\bm{\chi}_{t}+\bm{\xi}_{t}
where

\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{t}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{B}(L)\mathbf{u}_{t}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{B}_{\ell}\mathbf{u}_{t-\ell}\;\;\text{ with }\mathbf{u}_{t}=(u_{1t},\ldots,u_{qt})^{\top},\quad\text{and}

(1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(L)\bm{\xi}_{t}

\displaystyle=\bm{\xi}_{t}-\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}\mathbf{A}_{\ell}\bm{\xi}_{t-\ell}=\bm{\Gamma}^{1/2}\bm{\varepsilon}_{t}\;\;\text{ with }\bm{\varepsilon}_{t}=(\varepsilon_{1t},\ldots,\varepsilon_{pt})^{\top}.

(2)

In (
1
), the latent random vector
\mathbf{u}_{t}
, referred to as the vector of
common factors
or
common shocks
, is assumed to satisfy
\mathsf{E}(\mathbf{u}_{t})=\mathbf{0}
and
\mathsf{Cov}(\mathbf{u}_{t})=\mathbf{I}_{q}
, and
are loaded on each
\chi_{it}
via square summable, one-sided filters

\mathcal{B}_{ij}(L)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}B_{\ell,ij}L^{\ell}
, where
\mathbf{B}_{\ell}=[B_{\ell,ij},\,1\leq i\leq p,\,1\leq j\leq q]\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}
.
This defines the generalised dynamic factor model (GDFM) proposed by
Forni et al., 2000
and
Forni and Lippi, 2001
, which provides the most general approach to high-dimensional time series factor modelling.

In (
2
), the idiosyncratic component
\bm{\xi}_{t}
is modelled as a VAR(
d
) process for some finite positive integer
d
, with innovations
\bm{\Gamma}^{1/2}\bm{\varepsilon}_{t}

where
\bm{\Gamma}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
is some positive definite matrix and
\bm{\Gamma}^{1/2}
its symmetric square root matrix, and
\mathsf{E}(\bm{\varepsilon}_{t})=\mathbf{0}
and
\mathsf{Cov}(\bm{\varepsilon}_{t})=\mathbf{I}_{p}
.
We assume that
\bm{\xi}_{t}
is causal (see Assumption
2.3

(i)
below), i.e. it admits the Wold representation:

\displaystyle\bm{\xi}_{t}=\mathcal{D}(L)\bm{\Gamma}^{1/2}\bm{\varepsilon}_{t}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{D}_{\ell}\bm{\Gamma}^{1/2}\bm{\varepsilon}_{t-\ell}\text{ \ with \ }\mathcal{D}(L)=\mathcal{A}^{-1}(L),

(3)

such that
\bm{\Gamma}^{1/2}\bm{\varepsilon}_{t}
is seen as a vector of
idiosyncratic shocks
loaded on each
\xi_{it}
via square summable, one-sided filters
\mathcal{D}_{ik}(L)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}D_{\ell,ik}L^{\ell}
where
\mathbf{D}_{\ell}=[D_{\ell,ik},\,1\leq i,k\leq p]
.
After accounting for the dominant cross-sectional dependence in the data (both contemporaneous and lagged) by factors,
it is reasonable to assume that the dependences left in
\bm{\xi}_{t}
are weak and, therefore, that the VAR structure is sufficiently sparse.
Discussion on the precise requirement on the sparsity of
\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\,1\leq\ell\leq d
, and
\bm{\Gamma}^{-1}
is deferred to Section
3
.

Remark 2.1
.

A special case of the GDFM is the popularly adopted static factor model where the factors are loaded only contemporaneously (see e.g.
Stock and Watson, 2002
;
Bai, 2003
;
Fan et al., 2013
).
This is formalised in Assumption
4.1
below, where we consider forecasting under a static representation.
A sufficient condition to obtain a static representation from the GDFM in (
1
), is to assume
\mathcal{B}(L)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{s}\mathbf{B}_{\ell}L^{\ell}
for some finite integer
s\geq 0
.
For example, if
s=0
, the model reduces to
\bm{\chi}_{t}=\mathbf{B}_{0}\mathbf{u}_{t}
while if
s>0
, it can be written as
\bm{\chi}_{t}=\bm{\Lambda}\mathbf{F}_{t}
with
\bm{\Lambda}=[\mathbf{B}_{\ell},\,0\leq\ell\leq s]
and
\mathbf{F}_{t}=(\mathbf{u}_{t}^{\top},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{t-s}^{\top})^{\top}
.
Under the static factor model,
\mathbf{X}_{t}
admits a factor-augmented VAR representation (see Remark
4.1
below).

In the remainder of this section, we list the assumptions required for identification and estimation of (
1
)–(
2
).
Since
\bm{\chi}_{t}
and
\bm{\xi}_{t}
are latent, some assumptions are required to ensure their (asymptotic) identifiability which are made in the frequency domain.
Denote by
\bm{\Sigma}_{x}(\omega)
the spectral density matrix of
\mathbf{X}_{t}
at frequency
\omega\in[-\pi,\pi]
, and
\mu_{x,j}(\omega)
its dynamic eigenvalues which are real-valued and ordered in the decreasing order.
We similarly define
\bm{\Sigma}_{\chi}(\omega)
,
\mu_{\chi,j}(\omega)
,
\bm{\Sigma}_{\xi}(\omega)
and
\mu_{\xi,j}(\omega)
.

Assumption 2.1
.

There exist a positive integer
p_{0}\geq 1
,
constants
\rho_{j}\in(3/4,1]
with
\rho_{1}\geq\ldots\geq\rho_{q}
,
and pairs of continuous functions

\omega\mapsto\alpha_{\chi,j}(\omega)

and
\omega\mapsto\beta_{\chi,j}(\omega)

for
\omega\in[-\pi,\pi]
and
1\leq j\leq q
, such that
for all
p\geq p_{0}
,

\displaystyle\beta_{\chi,1}(\omega)\geq\frac{\mu_{\chi,1}(\omega)}{p^{\rho_{1}}}\geq\alpha_{\chi,1}(\omega)>\ldots>\beta_{\chi,q}(\omega)\geq\frac{\mu_{\chi,q}(\omega)}{p^{\rho_{q}}}\geq\alpha_{\chi,q}(\omega)>0.

Under the assumption, if
\rho_{j}=1
for all
1\leq j\leq q
,
then we are in presence of
q
factors that are equally pervasive for the whole cross-section. The left panel of Figure
1
depicts the case when
\rho_{1}=1
.
If
\rho_{j}<1
for some
j
, we permit the presence of ‘weak’ factors
and our theoretical analysis explicitly reflects this;
see
De Mol et al., 2008
,
Onatski, 2012
,
Freyaldenhoven, 2021
and
Uematsu and Yamagata, 2023b

for estimation under static factor models permitting weak factors.
When weak factors are present, the ordering of the variables becomes important as
p\to\infty
,
whereas the case of linearly diverging factor strengths is compatible with completely arbitrary cross-sectional ordering.
The requirement that
\rho_{j}>3/4
is a minimal one, and generally larger values of
\rho_{j}
are required as the dimensionality increases and heavier tails are permitted as discussed later.

Assumptions
2.2
and
2.3
are made to control the serial dependence in
\mathbf{X}_{t}
.

Assumption 2.2
.

There exist some constants
\Xi>0
and
\varsigma>2
such that for all
\ell\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq i\leq p}|\mathbf{B}_{\ell,i\cdot}|_{2}\leq\Xi(1+\ell)^{-\varsigma}\quad\text{and}\quad\left(\sum_{j=1}^{q}|\mathbf{B}_{\ell,\cdot j}|_{\infty}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\leq\Xi(1+\ell)^{-\varsigma}.

Assumption 2.3
.

(i)

d
is a finite positive integer and
\det(\mathcal{A}(z))\neq 0
for all
|z|\leq 1
.

(ii)

There exist some constants
0<m_{\varepsilon}\leq M_{\varepsilon}
such that

\|\bm{\Gamma}\|\leq M_{\varepsilon}
and
\Lambda_{\min}(\bm{\Gamma})\geq m_{\varepsilon}
.

(iii)

There exist a constant
m_{\xi}>0
such that

\inf_{\omega\in[-\pi,\pi]}\mu_{\xi,p}(\omega)\geq m_{\xi}
.

(iv)

There exist some constants
\Xi>0
and
\varsigma>2
such that for all
\ell\geq 0
,

\displaystyle|D_{\ell,ik}|\leq C_{ik}(1+\ell)^{-\varsigma}\text{ with }\max\left\{\max_{1\leq k\leq p}\sum_{i=1}^{p}C_{ik},\,\max_{1\leq i\leq p}\sum_{k=1}^{p}C_{ik},\max_{1\leq i\leq p}\sqrt{\sum_{k=1}^{p}C_{ik}^{2}}\right\}\leq\Xi.

Assumption
2.3

(i)
and
(ii)
are standard in the literature
(
Lütkepohl, 2005
)
and imply that
\bm{\xi}_{t}
is causal and has finite and non-zero covariance.
Under Assumptions
2.2
and
2.3

(iv)
(imposed on the Wold decomposition of
\bm{\xi}_{t}
in (
3
)), the serial dependence in
\mathbf{X}_{t}
decays at an algebraic rate.
Further, we obtain a uniform bound for
\mu_{\xi,j}(\omega)
under Assumption
2.3

(iv)
:

Proposition 2.1
.

Under Assumption
2.3
, uniformly over all
\omega\in[-\pi,\pi]
,
there exists some constant
B_{\xi}>0
depending only on
M_{\varepsilon}
,
\Xi
and
\varsigma
,
defined in Assumption
2.3

(iii)
and
(iv)
, such that

\sup_{\omega\in[-\pi,\pi]}\mu_{\xi,1}(\omega)\leq B_{\xi}
.

Remark 2.2
.

Proposition
2.1
and Assumption
2.3

(iii)
jointly establish the uniform boundedness of
\mu_{\xi,1}(\omega)
and
\mu_{\xi,p}(\omega)
, which is commonly assumed in the literature on high-dimensional VAR estimation via
\ell_{1}
-regularisation.
A sufficient condition for Assumption
2.3

(iii)
is that
\max\left\{\max_{1\leq i\leq p}\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}|\mathbf{A}_{\ell,i\cdot}|_{1},\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}|\mathbf{A}_{\ell,\cdot j}|_{1}\right\}~\leq~\Xi

for some constant
\Xi>0

(
Basu and Michailidis, 2015
)
.
Further, when e.g.
d=1
, Assumption
2.3

(iv)
follows if
|\mathbf{A}_{1}|_{\infty}\leq\gamma<1
since
\max(\|\mathbf{D}_{\ell}\|_{1},\|\mathbf{D}_{\ell}\|_{\infty})\leq\Xi\gamma^{\ell}
with
\mathbf{D}_{\ell}=\mathbf{A}_{1}^{\ell}
.

The two latent components
\bm{\chi}_{t}
and
\bm{\xi}_{t}
, and the number of factors
q
, are identified thanks to the large gap between the eigenvalues of their spectral density matrices, which follows from Assumption
2.1
and Proposition
2.1
.
Then by Weyl’s inequality, the
q
-th dynamic eigenvalue
\mu_{x,q}(\omega)

diverges almost everywhere in
[-\pi,\pi]
as
p\to\infty
, whereas
\mu_{x,q+1}(\omega)
is uniformly bounded for any
p\in\mathbb{N}
and
\omega
.
This property is exploited in the FNETS methodology as later described in Section
3.2
.
It is worth stressing that Assumption
2.1
and Proposition
2.1
jointly constitute both a necessary and sufficient condition for the process
\mathbf{X}_{t}
to admit the dynamic factor representation in (
1
), see
Forni and Lippi, 2001
.

Finally, we characterise the common and idiosyncratic innovations.

Assumption 2.4
.

(i)

\{\mathbf{u}_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a sequence of zero-mean,
q
-dimensional martingale difference vectors with
\mathsf{Cov}(\mathbf{u}_{t})=\mathbf{I}_{q}
, and
u_{it}
and
u_{jt}
are independent for all
1\leq i,j\leq q
with
i\neq j
and all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(ii)

\{\bm{\varepsilon}_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a sequence of zero-mean,
p
-dimensional martingale difference vectors with
\mathsf{Cov}(\bm{\varepsilon}_{t})=\mathbf{I}_{p}
, and
\varepsilon_{it}
and
\varepsilon_{jt}
are independent for all
1\leq i,j\leq p
with
i\neq j
and all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(iii)

\mathsf{E}(u_{jt}\varepsilon_{it^{\prime}})~=~0
for all
1\leq j\leq q
,
1\leq i\leq p
and
t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(iv)

There exist some constants
\nu>4
and
\mu_{\nu}>0
such that

\max\left\{\max_{1\leq j\leq q}\mathsf{E}(|u_{jt}|^{\nu}),\max_{1\leq i\leq p}\mathsf{E}(|\varepsilon_{it}|^{\nu})\right\}\leq\mu_{\nu}
.

Assumption
2.4

(i)
and
(ii)
allow the common and idiosyncratic innovations to be sequences of martingale differences, relaxing the assumption of serial independence found in
Forni et al., 2017
.
Condition
(iii)
is standard in the factor modelling literature.
Under
(iv)
, we require that the innovations have
\nu>4
moments, which is considerably weaker than the Gaussianity assumed in the literature on VAR modelling of high-dimensional time series
(
Basu and Michailidis, 2015
;
Han et al., 2015
)
.
In Appendix
F
, we separately consider the case when
\mathbf{u}_{t}
and
\bm{\varepsilon}_{t}
are Gaussian for for the sake of comparison.

3
Network estimation via FNETS

3.1
Networks underpinning factor-adjusted VAR processes

Under the latent VAR model in (
2
), we can define three types of networks
underpinning the interconnectedness of
\mathbf{X}_{t}
after factor adjustment
(
Barigozzi and Brownlees, 2019
)
.

Let
\mathcal{V}=\{1,\ldots,p\}
denote the set of vertices representing the
p
time series.
Firstly, the transition matrices
\mathbf{A}_{\ell}=[A_{\ell,ii^{\prime}},\,1\leq i,i^{\prime}\leq p]
, encode
the directed network
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{G}}}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}^{\text{{G}}})
representing Granger causal linkages, with

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\text{{G}}}=\left\{(i,i^{\prime})\in\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{V}:\,A_{\ell,ii^{\prime}}\neq 0\text{ for some }1\leq\ell\leq d\right\}

(4)

as the set of edges.
Here, the presence of an edge
(i,i^{\prime})\in\mathcal{E}^{\text{{G}}}

indicates that
\xi_{i^{\prime},t-\ell}
Granger causes
\xi_{it}
at some lag
1\leq\ell\leq d
.

The second network contains undirected edges representing
contemporaneous dependence between VAR innovations
\bm{\Gamma}^{1/2}\bm{\varepsilon}_{t}
,
denoted by
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{C}}}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}^{\text{{C}}})
;
we have
(i,i^{\prime})\in\mathcal{E}^{\text{{C}}}
iff the partial correlation between
the
i
-th and
i^{\prime}
-th elements of
\bm{\Gamma}^{1/2}\bm{\varepsilon}_{t}
is non-zero.
Specifically, letting
\bm{\Gamma}^{-1}=\bm{\Delta}=[\delta_{ii^{\prime}},\,1\leq i,i^{\prime}\leq p]
, the set of edges is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\text{{C}}}=\left\{(i,i^{\prime})\in\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{V}:\,i\neq i^{\prime}\text{ and }-\frac{\delta_{ii^{\prime}}}{\sqrt{\delta_{ii}\cdot\delta_{i^{\prime}i^{\prime}}}}\neq 0\right\}.

(5)

Finally, we summarise the aforementioned
lead-lag and contemporaneous relations between the variables
in a single, undirected network
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{L}}}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}^{\text{{L}}})

by means of the long-run partial correlations of
\bm{\xi}_{t}
.
Let
\bm{\Omega}=[\omega_{ii^{\prime}},\,1\leq i,i^{\prime}\leq p]
denote
the long-run partial covariance matrix of
\bm{\xi}_{t}
,
i.e.
\bm{\Omega}=(\bm{\Sigma}_{\xi}(0))^{-1}=2\pi\mathcal{A}^{\top}(1)\bm{\Delta}\mathcal{A}(1)
under (
2
).
Then, the set of edges of
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{L}}}
is

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\text{{L}}}=\left\{(i,i^{\prime})\in\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{V}:\,i\neq i^{\prime}\text{ and }-\frac{\omega_{ii^{\prime}}}{\sqrt{\omega_{ii}\cdot\omega_{i^{\prime}i^{\prime}}}}\neq 0\right\}.

(6)

Generally,
\mathcal{E}^{\text{{L}}}
is greater than
\mathcal{E}^{\text{{G}}}\cup\mathcal{E}^{\text{{C}}}
, see Appendix
C
for a sufficient condition for the absence of an edge
(i,i^{\prime})
from
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{L}}}
.
In the remainder of Section
3
, we describe the network estimation methodology of FNETS which, consisting of three steps, estimates the three networks while fully accounting for the challenges arising from not directly observing the VAR process
\bm{\xi}_{t}
, and investigate its theoretical properties.

3.2
Step 1: Factor adjustment via dynamic PCA

As described in Section
2
, under our model (
1
)–(
2
), there exists a large gap in
\mu_{x,j}(\omega)
, the dynamic eigenvalues of the spectral density matrix of
\mathbf{X}_{t}
, between those attributed to the factors (
j\leq q
) and those which are not (
j\geq q+1
).
With the goal of estimating the autocovariance (ACV) matrix of the latent VAR process
\bm{\xi}_{t}
, we exploit this gap in the factor-adjustment step based on dynamic principal component analysis (PCA);
see Chapter 9 of
Brillinger, 1981
for the definition of dynamic PCA and
Forni et al., 2000
for its use in the estimation of GDFM.
Throughout, we treat
q
as known and refer to
Hallin and Liška, 2007
for its consistent estimation under (
1
).

Denote the ACV matrices of
\mathbf{X}_{t}
by

\bm{\Gamma}_{x}(\ell)=\mathsf{E}(\mathbf{X}_{t-\ell}\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\top})
for
\ell\geq 0

and
\bm{\Gamma}_{x}(\ell)=\bm{\Gamma}_{x}^{\top}(-\ell)
for
\ell\leq-1
,
and analogously define
\bm{\Gamma}_{\chi}(\ell)
and
\bm{\Gamma}_{\xi}(\ell)

with
\bm{\chi}_{t}
and
\bm{\xi}_{t}
replacing
\mathbf{X}_{t}
, respectively.
Then,
\bm{\Sigma}_{x}(\omega)
and
\bm{\Gamma}_{x}(\ell)
satisfy

\bm{\Sigma}_{x}(\omega)=(2\pi)^{-1}\sum_{\ell=-\infty}^{\infty}\bm{\Gamma}_{x}(\ell)\exp(-\iota\ell\omega)

for all
\omega\in[-\pi,\pi]
. Motivated by this, we estimate
\bm{\Sigma}_{x}(\omega)
by

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{x}(\omega)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{\ell=-m}^{m}K\left(\frac{\ell}{m}\right)\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{x}(\ell)\exp(-\iota\ell\omega),

(7)

with the sample ACV
\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{x}(\ell)=n^{-1}\sum_{t=\ell+1}^{n}\mathbf{X}_{t-\ell}\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\top}
when
\ell\geq 0
, and
\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{x}(\ell)=\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{x}(-\ell)^{\top}
for
\ell<0
, and the kernel bandwidth
m=\lfloor n^{\beta}\rfloor
for some
\beta\in(0,1)
.
We adopt the Bartlett kernel as
K(\cdot)

which ensures positive semi-definiteness of
\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{x}(\omega)

(see Appendix
F.2.4
).
Then, we evaluate
\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{x}(\omega)
at the
2m+1
Fourier frequencies
\omega_{k},\,-m\leq k\leq m
(
\omega_{k}=2\pi k/(2m+1)
for
0\leq k\leq m
, and
\omega_{k}=-\omega_{|k|}
for
-m\leq k\leq-1
),
and estimate
\bm{\Sigma}_{\chi}(\omega_{k})
by retaining the contribution from the
q
largest eigenvalues and eigenvectors only.
That is, we obtain
\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\chi}(\omega_{k})=\sum_{j=1}^{q}\widehat{\mu}_{x,j}(\omega_{k})\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{x,j}(\omega_{k})(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{x,j}(\omega_{k}))^{*}
(with
*
denoting the transposed complex conjugate),
where
\widehat{\mu}_{x,1}(\omega)\geq\ldots\geq\widehat{\mu}_{x,q}(\omega)
, denote the
q
leading eigenvalues of
\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{x}(\omega)
and
\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{x,j}(\omega)
the associated (normalised) eigenvectors.
From this, an estimator of
\bm{\Gamma}_{\chi}(\ell)
at a given lag
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, is obtained via inverse Fourier transform as

\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\chi}(\ell)=2\pi(2m+1)^{-1}\sum_{k=-m}^{m}\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\chi}(\omega_{k})\exp(\iota\ell\omega_{k})

and finally, we estimate the ACV matrices of
\bm{\xi}_{t}
with
\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\xi}(\ell)=\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{x}(\ell)-\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\chi}(\ell)
, by virtue of Assumption
2.4

(iii)
.

3.3
Step 2: Estimation of VAR parameters and
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{G}}}

Recalling the VAR(
d
) model in (
2
), let
\bm{\beta}=[\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\,1\leq\ell\leq d]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{(pd)\times p}
denote the matrix collecting all the VAR parameters.
When
\bm{\xi}_{t}
is directly observable,
\ell_{1}
-regularised least squares or maximum likelihood estimators have been proposed for
\bm{\beta}
, see the references given in Introduction.
In the context of factor-adjusted regression modelling where the aim is to estimate the regression structure in the latent idiosyncratic process, it has been proposed to apply the
\ell_{1}
-regularisation methods after estimating the entire latent process by, say,
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}_{t}

(
Fan et al., 2020
;
Fan et al., 2021
;
Fan et al., 2023
;
Krampe and Margaritella, 2021
)
.
However, such an approach possibly suffers from the lack of statistical efficiency due to having to control the estimation errors in
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}_{t}
uniformly for all
1\leq t\leq n
.
Instead, we make use of the Yule-Walker (YW) equation
\bm{\beta}=\mathbbm{G}^{-1}\mathbbm{g}
, where

\displaystyle\mathbbm{G}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\Gamma}_{\xi}(0)&\bm{\Gamma}_{\xi}(-1)&\ldots&\bm{\Gamma}_{\xi}(-d+1)\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\bm{\Gamma}_{\xi}(d-1)&\bm{\Gamma}_{\xi}(d-2)&\ldots&\bm{\Gamma}_{\xi}(0)\end{bmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbbm{g}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\Gamma}_{\xi}(1)\\
\vdots\\
\bm{\Gamma}_{\xi}(d)\end{bmatrix},

with
\mathbbm{G}
being always invertible since
\Lambda_{\min}(\mathbbm{G})\geq 2\pi m_{\xi}>0

by Assumption
2.3

(iii)
. We propose to estimate
\bm{\beta}
as a regularised YW estimator based on
\widehat{\mathbbm{G}}
and
\widehat{\mathbbm{g}}
, which are obtained by replacing
\bm{\Gamma}_{\xi}(\ell)
with
\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\xi}(\ell)
derived in Step 1 of FNETS via dynamic PCA, in the definitions of
\mathbbm{G}
and
\mathbbm{g}
, respectively.

To handle the high dimensionality, we consider an
\ell_{1}
-regularised estimator for
\bm{\beta}
which solves the following
\ell_{1}
-penalised
M
-estimation problem

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\beta}}={\arg\min}_{\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{pd\times p}}\ \mathsf{tr}\left(\mathbf{M}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbbm{G}}\mathbf{M}-2\mathbf{M}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbbm{g}}\right)+\lambda|\mathbf{M}|_{1}

(8)

with a tuning parameter
\lambda>0
.
Note that the matrix
\widehat{\mathbbm{G}}
is guaranteed to be positive semi-definite
(see Appendix
F.2.4
),
thus the problem in (
8
) is convex with a global minimiser.
We note the similarity between (
8
) and the Lasso estimator, but our estimator is specifically tailored for the problem of estimating the parameters for the latent VAR process
\bm{\xi}_{t}
by means of second-order moments only, and thus differs fundamentally from the Lasso-type estimators proposed for high-dimensional VAR estimation.
In Appendix
A
, we propose an alternative estimator based on a constrained
\ell_{1}
-minimisation approach closely related to the Dantzig selector
(
Candes and Tao, 2007
)
.

Once the VAR parameters are estimated, we propose to estimate the edge set of
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{G}}}
in (
4
) by the set of indices of the non-zero elements of a thresholded version of
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
, denoted by
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}(\mathfrak{t})=[\widehat{\beta}_{ij}\cdot\mathbb{I}_{\{|\widehat{\beta}_{ij}|>\mathfrak{t}\}}]
, with some threshold
\mathfrak{t}>0
.

3.4
Step 3: Estimation of
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{C}}}
and
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{L}}}

Recall that the edge sets of
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{C}}}
and
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{L}}}
defined in (
5
)–(
6
), are given by the supports of
\bm{\Delta}
and
\bm{\Omega}
.
Given
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
in (
8
) which estimates
\bm{\beta}
, a natural estimator of
\bm{\Gamma}
arises from the YW equation

\bm{\Gamma}=\bm{\Gamma}_{\xi}(0)-\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}\mathbf{A}_{\ell}\bm{\Gamma}_{\xi}(\ell)=\bm{\Gamma}_{\xi}(0)-\bm{\beta}^{\top}\mathbbm{g}
,
as
\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}=\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\xi}(0)-\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbbm{g}}
.
Then, we propose to estimate
\bm{\Delta}=\bm{\Gamma}^{-1}
via constrained
\ell_{1}
-minimisation as

\displaystyle\check{\bm{\Delta}}

\displaystyle={\arg\min}_{\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}}|\mathbf{M}|_{1}\quad\text{subject to}\quad\left|\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{I}\right|_{\infty}\leq\eta,

(9)

where
\eta>0
is a tuning parameter.
This approach has originally been proposed for estimating the precision matrix of independent data
(
Cai et al., 2011
)
, which we extend to time series settings.
Since
\check{\bm{\Delta}}=[\check{\delta}_{ii^{\prime}},\,1\leq i,j\leq p]
is not guaranteed to be symmetric, a symmetrisation step is performed to obtain

\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}=[\widehat{\delta}_{ii^{\prime}},\,1\leq i,i^{\prime}\leq p]

with

\widehat{\delta}_{ii^{\prime}}=\check{\delta}_{ii^{\prime}}\cdot\mathbb{I}_{\{|\check{\delta}_{ii^{\prime}}|\leq|\check{\delta}_{i^{\prime}i}|\}}+\check{\delta}_{i^{\prime}i}\cdot\mathbb{I}_{\{|\check{\delta}_{i^{\prime}i}|<|\check{\delta}_{ii^{\prime}}|\}}
.
Then, the edge set of
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{C}}}
in (
5
) is estimated by the support of the thresholded estimator
\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\delta})=[\widehat{\delta}_{ii^{\prime}}\cdot\mathbb{I}_{\{|\widehat{\delta}_{ii^{\prime}}|>\mathfrak{t}_{\delta}\}},\,1\leq i,i^{\prime}\leq p]
with some threshold
\mathfrak{t}_{\delta}>0
.

Finally, we estimate
\bm{\Omega}=2\pi(\mathcal{A}(1))^{\top}\bm{\Delta}\mathcal{A}(1)
by replacing
\mathcal{A}(1)
and
\bm{\Delta}
with their estimators.
We adopt the thresholded estimator
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}(\mathfrak{t})=[\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{1}(\mathfrak{t}),\ldots,\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{d}(\mathfrak{t})]^{\top}
, to obtain
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}(1)=\mathbf{I}-\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{\ell}(\mathfrak{t})
and set
\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}=2\pi(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}(1))^{\top}\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}\widehat{\mathcal{A}}(1)
.
Analogously, the edge set of
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{L}}}
in (
6
) is obtained by thresholding
\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}=[\widehat{\omega}_{ii^{\prime}},\,1\leq i,i^{\prime}\leq p]
with some threshold
\mathfrak{t}_{\omega}>0
, as the support of
\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\omega})=[\widehat{\omega}_{ii^{\prime}}\cdot\mathbb{I}_{\{|\widehat{\omega}_{ii^{\prime}}|>\mathfrak{t}_{\omega}\}},\,1\leq i,i^{\prime}\leq p]
.

3.5
Theoretical properties

We prove the consistency of FNETS in network estimation by establishing the theoretical properties of each of its three steps in Sections
3.5.1
–
3.5.3
. Then in Section
3.5.4
, we present the results for a special case where
\bm{\chi}_{t}
admits a static representation,
n\asymp p
and
\mathsf{E}(|X_{it}|^{\nu})<\infty
for
\nu>8
, for ease of comparing our results to the existing ones.

Hereafter, we define

\displaystyle\psi_{n}=\left(\frac{m}{n^{1-2/\nu}}\vee\sqrt{\frac{m\log(m)}{n}}\right)\text{ and }\vartheta_{n,p}=\left(\frac{m(np)^{2/\nu}\log^{7/2}(p)}{n}\vee\sqrt{\frac{m\log(mp)}{n}}\right),

(10)

where the dependence of these quantities on
\nu
is omitted for simplicity.

3.5.1
Factor adjustment via dynamic PCA

We first establish the consistency of the dynamic PCA-based estimator of
\bm{\Gamma}_{\chi}(\ell)
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Suppose that Assumptions
2.1
,
2.2
,
2.3
and
2.4
are met. Then, for any finite positive integer
s\leq d
, as
n,p\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\max_{\ell:\,|\ell|\leq s}\frac{1}{p}\left\|\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\chi}(\ell)-\bm{\Gamma}_{\chi}(\ell)\right\|_{F}=

\displaystyle\,O_{P}\left(qp^{2(1-\rho_{q})}\left(\psi_{n}\vee\frac{1}{m}\vee\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\right)\right),

\displaystyle\max_{\ell:\,|\ell|\leq s}\left|\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\chi}(\ell)-\bm{\Gamma}_{\chi}(\ell)\right|_{\infty}=

\displaystyle\,O_{P}\left(qp^{2(1-\rho_{q})}\left(\vartheta_{n,p}\vee\frac{1}{m}\vee\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\right)\right).

Remark 3.1
.

(a)

Theorem
3.1
is complemented by Proposition
F.15
in Appendix which establishes the consistency of the spectral density matrix estimator
\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\chi}(\omega)
uniformly over
\omega\in[-\pi,\pi]
in both Frobenius and
\ell_{\infty}
-norms.

(b)

Both
\psi_{n}
and
\vartheta_{n,p}
in (
10
) increase with the bandwidth
m
.
It is possible to find
m
that minimises e.g.
(\vartheta_{n,p}\vee m^{-1})
which, roughly speaking, represents the bias-variance trade-off in the estimation of the spectral density matrix
\bm{\Sigma}_{x}(\omega)
.
For example, in light-tailed settings with large enough
\nu
,
the choice
m\asymp(n\log^{-1}(np))^{1/3}

leads to the minimal rate in
\ell_{\infty}
-norm
(\vartheta_{n,p}\vee m^{-1})\asymp(\log(np)/n)^{1/3}

which nearly matches the optimal non-parametric rate when using the Bartlett kernel as in (
7
)
(
Priestley, 1982
, p. 463)
.

(c)

Consistency in Frobenius norm depends on
\psi_{n}
which tends to zero as
n\to\infty
without placing any constraint on the relative rate of divergence between
n
and
p
.
Consistency in
\ell_{\infty}
-norm
is determined by
\vartheta_{n,p}
which depends on the interplay between the dimensionality and the tail behaviour.
Generally, the estimation error worsens as weaker factors are permitted (
\rho_{q}<1
in Assumption
2.1
) and as
p
grows, and also when
\nu
is small such that heavier tails are permitted.
Consider the case when all factors are strong (i.e.
\rho_{j}=1
).
If
p\asymp n
, then
\ell_{\infty}
-consistency holds with an appropriately chosen
m=n^{\beta},\,\beta\in(0,1)
, that leads to
\vartheta_{n,p}=o(1)
, provided that
\nu>4
.
When all moments of
u_{jt}
and
\varepsilon_{it}
exist, we achieve
\ell_{\infty}
-consistency even in the ultra high-dimensional case where
\log(p)=o(n)
.

From Theorem
3.1
, the following proposition immediately follows.

Proposition 3.2
.

Suppose that the conditions in Theorem
3.1
are met and let Assumption
2.1
hold with
\rho_{j}=1,\,1\leq j\leq q
.
Then,
\mathsf{P}(\mathcal{E}_{n,p})\to 1
as
n,p\to\infty
, where

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{n,p}

\displaystyle=\left\{\max_{-d\leq\ell\leq d}\left|\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\xi}(\ell)-\bm{\Gamma}_{\xi}(\ell)\right|_{\infty}\leq C_{\xi}\left(\vartheta_{n,p}\vee\frac{1}{m}\vee\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\right)\right\}.

(11)

for some constant
C_{\xi}>0
.

From Proposition
3.2
, we have
\ell_{\infty}
-consistency of
\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\xi}(\ell)
in the presence of strong factors. Although it is possible to trace the effect of weak factors on the estimation of
\bm{\Gamma}_{\xi}(\ell)
(see Corollary
F.17
), we make this simplifying assumption to streamline the presentation of the theoretical results of the subsequent Steps 2–3 of FNETS.

Remark 3.2
.

In Appendix
F.2.8
, we show that if
\bm{\chi}_{t}
admits the static representation discussed in Remark
2.1
, the rate in Proposition
3.2
is further improved as

\displaystyle\max_{\ell:\,|\ell|\leq d}\left|\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\xi}(\ell)-\bm{\Gamma}_{\xi}(\ell)\right|_{\infty}=O_{P}\left(\widetilde{\vartheta}_{n,p}\vee\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\right)\text{ with }\widetilde{\vartheta}_{n,p}=\left(\frac{p^{2/\nu}\log^{3}(p)}{n^{1-2/\nu}}\vee\sqrt{\frac{\log(p)}{n}}\right).

(12)

The term
\widetilde{\vartheta}_{n,p}
comes from bounding
\max_{\ell:\,|\ell|\leq d}|\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{x}(\ell)-\bm{\Gamma}_{x}(\ell)|_{\infty}
.
Hence, the improved rate in (
12
) is comparable to the rate attained when we directly observe
\bm{\xi}_{t}
apart from the presence of
p^{-1/2}
, which is due to the presence of latent factors; similar observations are made in Theorem 3.1 of
Fan et al., 2013
.

3.5.2
Estimation of VAR parameters and
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{G}}}

We measure the sparsity of
\bm{\beta}
by
s_{0,j}=|\bm{\beta}_{\cdot j}|_{0}
,
s_{0}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}s_{0,j}
and
s_{\text{in}}=\max_{1\leq j\leq p}s_{0,j}
. When
d=1
, the quantity
s_{\text{in}}
coincides with the maximum in-degree per node of
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{G}}}
.

Proposition 3.3
.

Suppose that
C_{\xi}s_{\text{in}}(\vartheta_{n,p}\vee m^{-1}\vee p^{-1/2})\leq\pi m_{\xi}/16
, where
m_{\xi}
is defined in Assumption
2.3

(iii)
.
Also, set
\lambda\geq 4C_{\xi}(\|\bm{\beta}\|_{1}+1)(\vartheta_{n,p}\vee m^{-1}\vee p^{-1/2})
in (
8
). Then, conditional on
\mathcal{E}_{n,p}
defined in (
11
), we have

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\left|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{\cdot j}-\bm{\beta}_{\cdot j}\right|_{2}\leq\frac{6\sqrt{s_{\text{in}}}\lambda}{\pi m_{\xi}},\quad\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\left|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{\cdot j}-\bm{\beta}_{\cdot j}\right|_{1}\leq\frac{24s_{\text{in}}\lambda}{\pi m_{\xi}}\quad\text{ and}

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\left|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{\cdot j}-\bm{\beta}_{\cdot j}\right|_{\infty}\leq\min\left(4\|\mathbbm{G}^{-1}\|_{1}\lambda,\frac{6\sqrt{s_{\text{in}}}\lambda}{\pi m_{\xi}}\right).

Following
Loh and Wainwright, 2012
, the proof of Proposition
3.3
proceeds by showing that, conditional on
\mathcal{E}_{n,p}
, the matrix
\widehat{\mathbbm{G}}
meets a restricted eigenvalue condition
(
Bickel et al., 2009
)
and the deviation bound is controlled as
|\widehat{\mathbbm{G}}\bm{\beta}-\widehat{\mathbbm{g}}|_{\infty}\leq\lambda/4
.
Then, thanks to Proposition
3.2
, as
n,p\to\infty
, the estimation errors of
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
in
\ell_{2}
,
\ell_{1}
- and
\ell_{\infty}
-norms, are bounded as in Proposition
3.3
with probability tending to one.

Remark 3.3
.

As noted in Remark
2.2
, the boundedness of
\mu_{\xi,j}(\omega)
follows from that of
\|\bm{\beta}\|_{1}=\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}|\mathbf{A}_{\ell,j\cdot}|_{1}
, in which case
\|\bm{\beta}\|_{1}
appearing in the assumed lower bound on
\lambda
, does not inflate the rate of the estimation errors.
In the light-tailed situation, with the optimal bandwidth
m\asymp(n\log^{-1}(np))^{1/3}
as specified in Remark
3.1

(b)
, it is required that
s_{\text{in}}=O((n\log^{-1}(np))^{1/3}\wedge\sqrt{p})
, which still allows the number of non-zero entries in
each
row of
\mathbf{A}_{\ell}
to grow with
p
.
Here, the exponent
1/3
in place of
1/2
often found in the literature, comes from adopting the most general approach to time series factor modelling which necessitates selecting a bandwidth for frequency domain-based factor adjustment.

For sign consistency of the Lasso estimator, the (almost) necessary and sufficient condition is the so-called irrepresentable condition
(
Zhao and Yu, 2006
)
, which is known to be highly stringent
(
Tardivel and Bogdan, 2022
)
.
Alternatively,
Medeiros and Mendes, 2016
propose an adaptive Lasso estimator with data-driven weights for high-dimensional VAR estimation when
\bm{\xi}_{t}
is directly observed.
Instead, we propose to additionally threshold
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
and obtain
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}(\mathfrak{t})
, whose support consistently estimates the edge set of
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{G}}}
.

Corollary 3.4
.

Suppose that the conditions of Proposition
3.3
are met.
If

\displaystyle\min_{(i,j):\,|\beta_{ij}|>0}|\beta_{ij}|>2\mathfrak{t}

(13)

with
\mathfrak{t}=\min(4\|\mathbbm{G}^{-1}\|_{1}\lambda,6\sqrt{s_{\text{in}}}\lambda/(\pi m_{\xi}))
, then
\text{{sign}}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}(\mathfrak{t}))=\text{{sign}}(\bm{\beta})
conditional on
\mathcal{E}_{n,p}
.

3.5.3
Estimation of
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{C}}}
and
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{L}}}

Let
s_{\delta}(\varrho)=\max_{1\leq i\leq p}\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{p}|\delta_{ii^{\prime}}|^{\varrho},\,\varrho\in[0,1)
, denote the (weak) sparsity of
\bm{\Delta}=[\delta_{ii^{\prime}},\,1\leq i,i^{\prime}\leq p]
.
Also, define
s_{\text{out}}=\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}|\mathbf{A}_{\ell,\cdot j}|_{0}
which, complementing
s_{\text{in}}
, represents the sparsity of the out-going edges of
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{G}}}
.
Analogously as in Proposition
3.3
, we establish deterministic guarantees for
\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}
conditional on
\mathcal{E}_{n,p}
.

Proposition 3.5
.

Suppose that the conditions in Propositions
3.3
are met, and set
\eta=Cs_{\text{in}}\|\bm{\Delta}\|_{1}(\|\bm{\beta}\|_{1}+1)(\vartheta_{n,p}\vee m^{-1}\vee p^{-1/2})
in (
9
), with
C
depending only on
C_{\xi}
and
m_{\xi}
.
Then, conditional on
\mathcal{E}_{n,p}
defined in (
11
), we have:

(i)

|\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}-\bm{\Delta}|_{\infty}\leq 4\|\bm{\Delta}\|_{1}\eta
and

\|\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}-\bm{\Delta}\|\leq 12s_{\delta}(\varrho)(4\|\bm{\Delta}\|_{1}\eta)^{1-\varrho}
.

(ii)

If also
s_{\text{out}}\mathfrak{t}\leq\|\mathcal{A}(1)\|_{1}
with
\mathfrak{t}
chosen as in Corollary
3.4
,
then,

\displaystyle\left|\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}-\bm{\Omega}\right|_{\infty}\leq 4\pi\|\mathcal{A}(1)\|_{1}\left(3\|\bm{\Delta}\|s_{\text{out}}\mathfrak{t}+16\|\mathcal{A}(1)\|_{1}\|\bm{\Delta}\|_{1}\eta\right).

Together with Assumption
2.3

(ii)
,
Proposition
3.5

(i)

indicates asymptotic positive definiteness of
\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}

provided that
\bm{\Delta}
is sufficiently sparse, as measured by
\|\bm{\Delta}\|_{1}
and
s_{\delta}(\varrho)
.
By definition,
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{L}}}
combines
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{G}}}
and
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{C}}}
and consequently, its sparsity structure is determined by the sparsity of the other two networks, which is reflected in Proposition
3.5

(ii)
.
Specifically, the term
\|\mathcal{A}(1)\|_{1}
is related to the out-going property of
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{G}}}
, and satisfies
\|\mathcal{A}(1)\|_{1}\leq\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}|\mathbf{A}_{\ell,\cdot j}|_{1}
, where the boundedness of the right-hand side is sufficient for the boundedness of
\mu_{\xi,j}(\omega)
(Remark
2.2
).
Also,
\|\bm{\Delta}\|_{1}
reflects the sparsity of the edge set of
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{C}}}
, and the tuning parameter
\eta
depends on the sparsity of the in-coming edges of
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{G}}}
through
\|\bm{\beta}\|_{1}=\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}|\mathbf{A}_{\ell,j\cdot}|_{1}
and
s_{\text{in}}
.

Similarly as in Corollary
3.4
, we can show the consistency of the thresholded estimators
\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\delta})
and
\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\omega})
in estimating the edge sets of
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{C}}}
and
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{L}}}
, respectively.

Corollary 3.6
.

Suppose that conditions of Proposition
3.5
are met.
Conditional on
\mathcal{E}_{n,p}
:

(i)

If
\min_{(i,i^{\prime}):\,|\delta_{ii^{\prime}}|>0}|\delta_{ii^{\prime}}|>2\mathfrak{t}_{\delta}
with
\mathfrak{t}_{\delta}=4\|\bm{\Delta}\|_{1}\eta
, we have
\text{sign}(\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\delta}))=\text{sign}(\bm{\Delta})
.

(ii)

If
\min_{(i,i^{\prime}):\,|\omega_{ii^{\prime}}|>0}|\omega_{ii^{\prime}}|>2\mathfrak{t}_{\omega}
with
\mathfrak{t}_{\omega}=4\pi\|\mathcal{A}(1)\|_{1}(3\|\bm{\Delta}\|s_{\text{out}}\mathfrak{t}+16\|\mathcal{A}(1)\|_{1}\|\bm{\Delta}\|_{1}\eta)
,

we have
\text{sign}(\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}(\mathfrak{t}_{\omega}))=\text{sign}(\bm{\Omega})
.

3.5.4
The case of the static factor model

For ease of comparing the performance of FNETS with the existing results, we focus on the static factor model setting discussed in Remark
2.1
, and assume that
n\asymp p
and
\max(\|\bm{\beta}\|_{1},\|\mathcal{A}(1)\|_{1})=O(1)
.
Then, from Remark
3.2
and the proof of Proposition
3.3
, we obtain
\max_{1\leq j\leq p}|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{\cdot j}-\bm{\beta}_{\cdot j}|_{2}=O_{P}(\sqrt{s_{\text{in}}\log(n)/n})
provided that
\nu>8
, such that the condition in (
13
) is written with
\mathfrak{t}\asymp\sqrt{s_{\text{in}}\log(n)/n}
. That is,
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
and its thresholded counterpart proposed for the estimation of the latent VAR process, perform as well as the benchmark derived under independence and Gaussianity in the Lasso literature
(
van de Geer et al., 2011
)
.
In this same setting, the factor-adjusted regression estimation method of
Fan et al., 2021
, when applied to the problem of VAR parameter estimation, yields an estimator
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\text{FARM}}
which attains
\max_{1\leq j\leq p}|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\text{FARM}}_{\cdot j}-\bm{\beta}_{\cdot j}|_{2}=O_{P}(\sqrt{s_{\text{in}}}n^{-1/2+5/\nu})
under strong mixingness, see their Theorem 3.
Here, the larger
O_{P}
-bound compared to ours stems from that
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\text{FARM}}
requires the estimation of
\xi_{it}
for all
i
and
t
, the error from which increases with
n
as well as
p
. This demonstrates the efficacy of adopting our regularised YW estimator.

Continuing with the same setting, Propositions
3.5
implies that

\displaystyle\left|\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}-\bm{\Delta}\right|_{\infty}=O_{P}\left(\|\bm{\Delta}\|_{1}^{2}s_{\text{in}}\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}\right)\text{ and }\left|\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}-\bm{\Omega}\right|_{\infty}=O_{P}\left(\left(s_{\text{out}}\vee\|\bm{\Delta}\|_{1}^{2}\sqrt{s_{\text{in}}}\right)\sqrt{\frac{s_{\text{in}}\log(n)}{n}}\right).

The former is comparable (up to
s_{\text{in}}
) to the results in Theorem 4 of
Cai et al., 2011
derived for estimating a sparse precision matrix of independent random vectors.

4
Forecasting via FNETS

4.1
Forecasting under the static factor model representation

For given time horizon
a\geq 0
, the best linear predictor of
\bm{\chi}_{n+a}

based on
\bm{\chi}_{n-\ell},\,\ell\geq 0
, is

\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{n+a|n}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{B}_{\ell+a}\mathbf{u}_{n-\ell}.

(14)

under (
1
).
Following
Forni et al., 2005
, we consider a forecasting method for the factor-driven component which estimates
\bm{\chi}_{n+a|n}
under a
restricted
GDFM that admits a static representation of finite dimension.
We formalise the static factor model discussed in Remark
2.1
in the following assumption.

Assumption 4.1
.

(i)

There exist two finite positive integers
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
such that
m_{1}+1\geq m_{2}
,
\bm{\chi}_{t}=\mathcal{M}^{(1)}(L)\mathbf{f}_{t}
and
\mathbf{f}_{t}=\mathcal{M}^{(2)}(L)\mathbf{u}_{t}

where
\mathcal{M}^{(1)}(L)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{m_{1}}\mathbf{M}^{(1)}_{\ell}L^{\ell}
with
\mathbf{M}^{(1)}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}
,
\mathcal{M}^{(2)}(L)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{m_{2}}\mathbf{M}^{(2)}_{\ell}L^{\ell}
with
\mathbf{M}^{(2)}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times q}
and
\det(\mathcal{M}^{(2)}(z))\neq 0
for all
|z|\leq 1
.

(ii)

Let
\mu_{\chi,j},\,1\leq j\leq r
, denote the
j
-th largest eigenvalue of
\bm{\Gamma}_{\chi}(0)
.
Then, there exist a positive integer
p_{0}\geq 1
,
constants
\varrho_{j}\in(7/8,1]
with
\varrho_{1}\geq\ldots\geq\varrho_{r}
,
and pairs of positive constants
(\alpha_{\chi,j},\beta_{\chi,j}),\,1\leq j\leq r
,
such that for all
p\geq p_{0}
,

\displaystyle\beta_{\chi,1}\geq\frac{\mu_{\chi,1}}{p^{\varrho_{1}}}\geq\alpha_{\chi,1}>\beta_{\chi,2}\geq\frac{\mu_{\chi,2}}{p^{\varrho_{2}}}\geq\ldots\geq\alpha_{\chi,r-1}>\beta_{\chi,r}\geq\frac{\mu_{\chi,r}}{p^{\varrho_{r}}}\geq\alpha_{\chi,r}>0.

In part
(i)
,
\bm{\chi}_{t}
admits a static representation with
r=q(m_{1}+1)
factors:
\bm{\chi}_{t}=\bm{\Lambda}\mathbf{F}_{t}
, where

\bm{\Lambda}=[\mathbf{M}^{(1)}_{\ell},\,0\leq\ell\leq m_{1}]
,
\mathbf{F}_{t}=(\mathbf{f}_{t}^{\top},\ldots,\mathbf{f}_{t-m_{1}}^{\top})^{\top}
and
\mathbf{f}_{t}=\mathcal{M}^{(2)}(L)\mathbf{u}_{t}
. The condition that
m_{1}+1\geq m_{2}
is made for convenience, and the proposed estimator of
\bm{\chi}_{n+a|n}
can be modified accordingly when it is relaxed.

Remark 4.1
.

Under Assumption
4.1

(i)
, the
r
-vector of static factors,
\mathbf{F}_{t}
, is driven by the
q
-dimensional common shocks
\mathbf{u}_{t}
.
If
q<r
,
Anderson and Deistler, 2008
show that
\mathbf{F}_{t}
always admits a VAR(
h
) representation:

\mathbf{F}_{t}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{h}\mathbf{G}_{\ell}\mathbf{F}_{t-\ell}+\mathbf{H}\mathbf{u}_{t}
for some finite positive integer
h
and
\mathbf{H}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times q}
.
Then,
\mathbf{X}_{t}
has a factor-augmented VAR representation:

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}_{t}

\displaystyle=\bm{\Lambda}\sum_{\ell=1}^{h}\mathbf{G}_{\ell}\mathbf{F}_{t-\ell}+\bm{\Lambda}\mathbf{H}\mathbf{u}_{t}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}\mathbf{A}_{\ell}\bm{\xi}_{t-\ell}+\bm{\Gamma}^{1/2}\bm{\varepsilon}_{t}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{d\vee h}\mathbf{C}_{\ell}\mathbf{F}_{t-\ell}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}\mathbf{A}_{\ell}\mathbf{X}_{t-\ell}+\bm{\nu}_{t},

with
\mathbf{C}_{\ell}=\bm{\Lambda}\mathbf{G}_{\ell}\mathbb{I}_{\{\ell\leq h\}}-\mathbf{A}_{\ell}\bm{\Lambda}\mathbb{I}_{\{\ell\leq d\}}
and
\bm{\nu}_{t}=\bm{\Lambda}\mathbf{H}\mathbf{u}_{t}+\bm{\Gamma}^{1/2}\bm{\varepsilon}_{t}
.
This model is a generalisation of the factor augmented forecasting model considered by
Stock and Watson, 2002
where only the factor-driven component is present, and it is also considered by
Fan et al., 2021
.

It immediately follows from Proposition
2.1
that
\|\bm{\Gamma}_{\xi}(0)\|\leq 2\pi B_{\xi}
.
This, combined with Assumption
4.1

(ii)
,
indicates the presence of a large gap in the eigenvalues of
\bm{\Gamma}_{x}(0)
,
which allows the asymptotic identification of
\bm{\chi}_{t}
and
\bm{\xi}_{t}
in the time domain, as well as that of the number of static factors
r
.
Throughout, we treat
r
as known, and refer to e.g.
Bai and Ng, 2002
;
Onatski, 2010
;
Ahn and Horenstein, 2013
;
Trapani, 2018
, for its estimation.

Let
(\mu_{\chi,j},\mathbf{e}_{\chi,j}),\,1\leq j\leq r
, denote the pairs of eigenvalues and eigenvectors of
\bm{\Gamma}_{\chi}(0)
ordered such that
\mu_{\chi,1}\geq\ldots\geq\mu_{\chi,r}
.
Then,
\bm{\Gamma}_{\chi}(0)=\mathbf{E}_{\chi}\bm{\mathcal{M}}_{\chi}\mathbf{E}_{\chi}^{\top}
with
\bm{\mathcal{M}}_{\chi}=\text{diag}(\mu_{\chi,j},\,1\leq j\leq r)
and
\mathbf{E}_{\chi}=[\mathbf{e}_{\chi,j},\,1\leq j\leq r]
.
Under Assumption
4.1

(i)
, we have

\bm{\chi}_{n+a|n}
in (
14
) satisfy

\bm{\chi}_{n+a|n}=\text{Proj}\left(\bm{\chi}_{n+a}|\mathbf{F}_{n-\ell},\ell\geq 0\right)=\text{Proj}\left(\bm{\chi}_{n+a}|\mathbf{F}_{n}\right)=\bm{\Gamma}_{\chi}(-a)\mathbf{E}_{\chi}\bm{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}_{\chi}\mathbf{E}_{\chi}^{\top}\bm{\chi}_{n}
,
where
\text{Proj}(\cdot|\mathbf{z})
denotes the linear projection operator
onto the linear space spanned by
\mathbf{z}
.
When
a=0
, we trivially have
\bm{\chi}_{t|n}=\bm{\chi}_{t}
for
t\leq n
.
Then, a natural estimator of
\bm{\chi}_{n+a|n}
is

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\chi}}^{\text{{res}}}_{n+a|n}=\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\chi}(-a)\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\chi}\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{M}}}_{\chi}^{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\chi}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{n},

(15)

where
(\widehat{\mu}_{\chi,j},\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{\chi,j}),\,1\leq j\leq r
, denote the pairs of eigenvalues and eigenvectors of
\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\chi}(0)
, and
\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\chi}(\ell),\,\ell\in\{0,a\}
, are estimated as described in Section
3.2
.
As a by-product, we obtain the in-sample estimator by setting
a=0
, as

\widehat{\bm{\chi}}^{\text{{res}}}_{t}=\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\chi}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\chi}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{t}
for
1\leq t\leq n
.

Remark 4.2
.

Our proposed estimator
\widehat{\bm{\chi}}^{\text{{res}}}_{n+a|n}
differs from that of
Forni et al., 2005
, as they estimate the factor space via generalised PCA on
\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\chi}(0)
.
This in effect replaces
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\chi}
in (
15
) with the eigenvectors of
\mathbf{W}^{-1}\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\chi}(0)
where
\mathbf{W}
is a diagonal matrix containing the estimators of the sample variance of
\bm{\xi}_{t}
.
Such an approach may gain in efficiency compared to ours in the same way a weighted least squares estimator is more efficient than the ordinary one in the presence of heteroscedasticity.
However, since we investigate the consistency of
\widehat{\bm{\chi}}^{\text{{res}}}_{n+a|n}

without deriving its asymptotic distribution, we do not explore such approach in this paper.

In Appendix
B
, we present an alternative forecasting method that operates under an
unrestricted
GDFM, i.e. it does not require Assumption
4.1
.
Referred to as
\widehat{\bm{\chi}}^{\text{{unr}}}_{n+a|n}
, we compare its performance with that of

\widehat{\bm{\chi}}^{\text{{res}}}_{n+a|n}
in numerical studies.

Once VAR parameters are estimated by
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}=[\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{d}]^{\top}
as in (
8
), we produce a forecast of
\bm{\xi}_{n+a}
given
\mathbf{X}_{t},\,t\leq n
, by estimating the best linear predictor
\bm{\xi}_{n+a|n}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}\mathbf{A}_{\ell}\bm{\xi}_{n+1-\ell|n}
(with
\bm{\xi}_{t|n}=\bm{\xi}_{t}
for
t\leq n
), as

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\xi}}_{n+a|n}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\max(1,a)-1}\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{\ell}\widehat{\bm{\xi}}_{n+a-\ell|n}+\sum_{\ell=\max(1,a)}^{d}\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{\ell}\widehat{\bm{\xi}}_{n+a-\ell}.

(16)

When
a\leq d
, the in-sample estimators appearing in (
16
) are obtained as
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}_{t}=\mathbf{X}_{t}-\widehat{\bm{\chi}}_{t},\,n+a-d\leq t\leq n
, with either
\widehat{\bm{\chi}}_{t}^{\text{{res}}}
or
\widehat{\bm{\chi}}_{t}^{\text{{unr}}}
as
\widehat{\bm{\chi}}_{t}
.

4.2
Theoretical properties

Proposition
4.1
establishes the consistency of
\widehat{\bm{\chi}}^{\text{{res}}}_{n+a|n}
in estimating the best linear predictor of
\bm{\chi}_{n+a}
, where we make it explicit the effects of the presence of weak factors, both dynamic (as measured by
\mu_{\chi,j}(\omega)
in Assumption
2.1
) and static (as measured by
\mu_{\chi,j}
in Assumption
4.1

(ii)
),
and the tail behaviour (through
\psi_{n}
and
\vartheta_{n,p}
defined in (
10
)).

Proposition 4.1
.

Suppose that the conditions in Theorem
3.1
are met
and, in addition, we assume that Assumption
4.1
holds.
Then, for any finite
a\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\widehat{\bm{\chi}}^{\text{{res}}}_{n+a|n}-\bm{\chi}_{n+a|n}\right|_{\infty}=O_{P}\left(p^{4-2\rho_{q}-2\varrho_{r}}\left(\psi_{n}\vee p^{\varrho_{r}-1}\vartheta_{n,p}\vee\frac{1}{m}\vee\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\right)\right).

As noted in Remark
3.1

(c)
, weaker factors and heavier tails impose a stronger requirement on the dimensionality
p
.
If all factors are strong (
\varrho_{r}=1
), the rate becomes
(\vartheta_{n,p}\vee m^{-1}\vee p^{-1/2})
.
When
a=0
, Proposition
4.1
provides in-sample estimation consistency for any given
t\leq n
.
The next proposition accounts for the irreducible error in
\bm{\chi}_{n+a|n}
, with which we conclude the analysis of the forecasting error
|\widehat{\bm{\chi}}_{n+a|n}^{\text{{res}}}-\bm{\chi}_{n+a}|_{\infty}
when
a\geq 1
.

Proposition 4.2
.

Suppose that Assumptions
2.2
and
2.4
hold.
Then for any finite
a\geq 1
,
|\bm{\chi}_{n+a|n}-\bm{\chi}_{n+a}|_{\infty}=O_{P}(q^{1/\nu}\mu_{\nu}^{1/\nu}\log^{1/2}(p))
.

Recall the definition of
s_{\text{in}}
given in Section
3.5
.
The next proposition investigates the performance of
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}_{n+a|n}
when
a=1
, which can easily be extended to any finite
a\geq 2
.

Proposition 4.3
.

Suppose that the in-sample estimator of
\bm{\xi}_{t}
and
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
satisfy

\displaystyle\left|\widehat{\bm{\xi}}_{n+1-\ell}-\bm{\xi}_{n+1-\ell}\right|_{\infty}=O_{P}\left(\bar{\zeta}_{n,p}\right)\text{ \ for $1\leq\ell\leq d$}\text{ \ and \ }\left\|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}-\bm{\beta}\right\|_{1}=O_{P}(s_{\text{in}}\zeta_{n,p}).

(17)

Also,
let Assumptions
2.3
and
2.4
hold.
Then,

\displaystyle\left|\widehat{\bm{\xi}}_{n+1|n}-\bm{\xi}_{n+1}\right|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(s_{\text{in}}\zeta_{n,p}\left(\log^{1/2}(p)p^{1/\nu}\mu_{\nu}^{1/\nu}+\bar{\zeta}_{n,p}\right)+\|\bm{\beta}\|_{1}\bar{\zeta}_{n,p}+p^{1/\nu}\mu_{\nu}^{1/\nu}\right).

Either of the in-sample estimators
\widehat{\bm{\chi}}^{\text{{res}}}_{t}
(described in Section
4.1
) or
\widehat{\bm{\chi}}^{\text{{unr}}}_{t}
(Appendix
B
), can be used in place of
\widehat{\bm{\chi}}_{t}
.
Accordingly, the rate
\bar{\zeta}_{n,p}
in (
17
) is inherited by that of
\widehat{\bm{\chi}}^{\text{{res}}}_{t}
(given in Proposition
4.1
) or
\widehat{\bm{\chi}}^{\text{{unr}}}_{t}
(Proposition
B.2

(iii)
).
From the proof of Proposition
3.3
, we have
\zeta_{n,p}\asymp(\|\bm{\beta}\|_{1}+1)(\vartheta_{n,p}\vee m^{-1}\vee p^{-1/2})
in (
17
).

5
Numerical studies

5.1
Tuning parameter selection

We briefly discuss the choice of the tuning parameters for FNETS.
For full details, see
Owens et al., 2023
.

Related to
\bm{\chi}_{t}
.

We set the kernel bandwidth at
m=\lfloor 4(n/\log(n))^{1/3}\rfloor
based on the case when sufficiently large number of moments exist and
n\asymp p
(Remark
3.1

(b)
).
In simulation studies reported in Appendix
E
, we treat the number of factors
q
(required for Step 1 of FNETS) known, and also treat the number of static factors
r
(for generating the forecast) as known if it is finite;
when
\bm{\chi}_{t}
does not admit a static factor model (i.e.
r=\infty
), we use the value returned by the ratio-based estimator of
Ahn and Horenstein, 2013
.
In real data analysis reported in Section
5.3
, we estimate both
q
and
r
, the former with the estimator proposed in
Hallin and Liška, 2007
, the latter as in
Ahn and Horenstein, 2013
.

Related to
\bm{\xi}_{t}
.

We select the tuning parameter
\lambda
in (
8
) jointly with the VAR order
d
, by adopting cross validation (CV); in time series settings, a similar approach is explored in
Wang and Tsay, 2022
.
For this, the data is partitioned into
M
consecutive folds with indices
\mathcal{I}_{l}=\{n_{l}+1,\ldots,n_{l+1}\}
where
n_{l}=\min(l\lceil n/M\rceil,n),\,0\leq l\leq M
, and each fold is split into
\mathcal{I}^{\text{train}}_{l}=\{n_{l}+1,\ldots,\lceil(n_{l}+n_{l+1})/2\rceil\}
and
\mathcal{I}^{\text{test}}_{l}=\mathcal{I}_{l}\setminus\mathcal{I}^{\text{train}}_{l}
.
Then with
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\text{train}}_{l}(\mu,b)
obtained from
\{\mathbf{X}_{t},\,t\in\mathcal{I}^{\text{train}}_{l}\}
with the tuning parameter
\mu
and the VAR order
b
, we evaluate

\displaystyle\text{CV}(\mu,b)=\sum_{l=1}^{M}

\displaystyle\,\mathsf{tr}\left(\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}^{\text{test}}_{\xi,l}(0)-(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{l}^{\text{train}}(\mu,b))^{\top}\widehat{\mathbbm{g}}_{l}^{\text{test}}(b)-(\widehat{\mathbbm{g}}_{l}^{\text{test}}(b))^{\top}\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{l}^{\text{train}}(\mu,b)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{l}^{\text{train}}(\mu,b))^{\top}\widehat{\mathbbm{G}}_{l}^{\text{test}}(b)\widehat{\bm{\beta}}_{l}^{\text{train}}(\mu,b)\right),

where
\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}^{\text{test}}_{\xi,l}(\ell)
,
\widehat{\mathbbm{G}}_{l}^{\text{test}}(b)
and
\widehat{\mathbbm{g}}_{l}^{\text{test}}(b)
are generated analogously as
\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\xi}(\ell)
,
\widehat{\mathbbm{G}}
and
\widehat{\mathbbm{g}}
, respectively, using the test set
\{\mathbf{X}_{t},\,t\in\mathcal{I}^{\text{test}}_{l}\}
.
The measure
\text{CV}(\mu,b)
approximates the prediction error while accounting for that we do not directly observe
\bm{\xi}_{t}
.
Minimising it over varying
\mu
and
b
, we select
\lambda
and
d
.
In simulation studies, we treat
d
as known
while in real data analysis, we select it from the set
\{1,\ldots,5\}
via CV.
For selecting
\eta
in (
9
), we adopt the Burg matrix divergence-based CV measure:

\displaystyle\text{CV}(\mu)=\sum_{l=1}^{M}\mathsf{tr}\left(\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}_{l}^{\text{train}}(\mu)\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{l}^{\text{test}}\right)-\log\left|\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}_{l}^{\text{train}}(\mu)\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{l}^{\text{test}}\right|-p.

For both CV procedures, we set
M=1
in the numerical results reported below.
In simulation studies, we compare the estimators with their thresholded counterparts in estimating the network edge sets with the thresholds
\mathfrak{t}
,
\mathfrak{t}_{\delta}
and
\mathfrak{t}_{\omega}
selected according to a data-driven approach motivated by
Liu et al., 2021
.
Details are in Appendix
D
.

5.2
Simulations

In Appendix
E
, we investigate the estimation and forecasting performance of FNETS ondatasets simulated under a variety of settings, from Gaussian innovations
\mathbf{u}_{t}
and
\bm{\varepsilon}_{t}
with
(E1)

\bm{\Delta}=\mathbf{I}
and
(E2)

\bm{\Delta}\neq\mathbf{I}
, to
(E3)
heavy-tailed (
t_{5}
) innovations with
\bm{\Delta}=\mathbf{I}
,
and when
\bm{\chi}_{t}
is generated from
(C1)
fully dynamic or
(C2)
static factor models.
In addition, we consider the ‘oracle’ setting
(C0)

\bm{\chi}_{t}=\mathbf{0}
where, in the absence of the factor-driven component, the results obtained can serve as a benchmark.
For comparison, we consider the factor-adjusted regression method of
Fan et al., 2021
and present the performance of their estimator of VAR parameters and forecasts.

5.3
Application to a panel of volatility measures

We investigate the interconnectedness in a panel of volatility measures
and evaluate its out-of-sample forecasting performance using FNETS.
For this purpose, we consider a panel of
p=46
stock prices
retrieved from the Wharton Research Data Service,
of US companies which are all classified as ‘financials’ according
to the Global Industry Classification Standard;
a list of company names and industry groups are found in Appendix
G
.
The dataset spans the period between January 3, 2000 and December 31, 2012 (
3267
trading days).
Following
Diebold and Yılmaz, 2014
, we measure the volatility using the high-low range as

\sigma_{it}^{2}=0.361(p^{\text{high}}_{it}-p^{\text{low}}_{it})^{2}

where
p^{\text{high}}_{it}
and
p^{\text{low}}_{it}
denote, respectively,
the maximum and the minimum log-price of stock
i
on day
t
,
and set
X_{it}=\log(\sigma_{it}^{2})
;
Brownlees and Gallo, 2010
support this choice of volatility measure over more sophisticated alternatives.

5.3.1
Network analysis

We focus on the period 03/2006–02/2010 corresponding to the Great Financial Crisis.
We partition the data into four segments of length
n=252
each
(corresponding to the number of trading days in a single year)
and on each segment, we apply FNETS to estimate the three networks

\mathcal{N}^{\text{{G}}}
,
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{C}}}
and
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{L}}}
described in Section
3.1
.

Each row of Figure
2
plots the heat maps of the matrices
underlying the three networks of interest.
From all four segments, the CV-based approach described in Section
5.1
returns
d=1
from the candidate VAR order set
\{1,\ldots,5\}
.
Hence in each row, the left panel represents the estimator
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{1}=\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\top}
,
and the middle and the right show the (long-run) partial correlations from the corresponding
\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}
(with their diagonals set to be zero).
Locations of the non-zero elements estimate the edge sets of the corresponding networks, and the hues represent the (signed) edge weights.

Prior to March 2007, all networks exhibit a low degree of interconnectedness
but the number of edges increases considerably in 03/2007–02/2008
due mainly to an overall increase in dynamic co-dependencies
and a prominent role of banks (blue group)
not only in
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{G}}}
but also in
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{C}}}
.
In 03/2008–02/2009, the companies belonging to the insurance sector (red group)
play a central role and in 03/2009–02/2010,
the companies become highly interconnected
with two particular firms having many outgoing edges in
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{G}}}
.
Also, while most edges in
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{L}}}
, which captures the overall long-run dependence,
have positive weights across time and companies,
their weights become negative in this last segment.
We highlight that FNETS is able to capture the aforementioned group-specific activities although this information is not supplied to the estimation method.

,

,

Forecasting

(

)
forecasts.
