We perform a rolling window-based forecasting exercise
on the trading days in
2012
.
Starting from
T=3016
(the first trading day in
2012
), we forecast
\mathbf{X}_{T+1}
as

\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{T+1|T}(n)=\widehat{\bm{\chi}}_{T+1|T}(n)+\widehat{\bm{\xi}}_{T+1|T}(n)
,
where
\widehat{\bm{\chi}}_{T+1|T}(n)
(resp.
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}_{T+1|T}(n)
)
denotes the forecast of
\bm{\chi}_{T+1}
(resp.
\bm{\xi}_{T+1}
) using
the preceding
n
data points
\{\mathbf{X}_{t},\,T-n+1\leq t\leq T\}
. We set
n=252
.
After the forecast
\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{T+1|T}(n)
is generated,
we update
T\leftarrow T+1
and repeat the above procedure until
T=3267

(the last trading day in
2012
) is reached.

For
\widehat{\bm{\chi}}_{T+1|T}(n)
, we consider the forecasting methods
derived under the static factor model (Section
4.1
, denoted by
\widehat{\bm{\chi}}^{\text{{res}}}_{T+1|T}(n)
)
and unrestricted GDFM (Appendix
B
,
\widehat{\bm{\chi}}^{\text{{unr}}}_{T+1|T}(n)
).
Following the analysis in Section
5.3.1
, we set
d=1
when producing
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}_{T+1|T}(n)
.
Additionally, we report the forecasting performance of FarmPredict
(
Fan et al., 2021
)
, which first fits an AR model to each of the
p
series (‘AR’), projects the residuals on their principal components, and then fits VAR models to what remains via Lasso.
Combining the three steps gives the final forecast
\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\text{FARM}}_{T+1|T}(n)
.
The forecast produced by the first step univariate AR modelling,
denoted by
\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\text{AR}}_{T+1|T}(n)
, is also included for comparison.

We evaluate the performance of
\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{T+1|T}

using two measures of errors

\text{FE}^{\text{avg}}_{T+1}=|\mathbf{X}_{T+1}|_{2}^{-2}\cdot|\mathbf{X}_{T+1}-\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{T+1|T}|_{2}^{2}
and

\text{FE}^{\text{max}}_{T+1}=|\mathbf{X}_{T+1}|_{\infty}^{-1}\cdot|\mathbf{X}_{T+1}-\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{T+1|T}|_{\infty}
,
see Table
1
for the summary of the forecasting results.
Among the forecasts generated by FNETS, the one based on
\widehat{\bm{\chi}}^{\text{{res}}}_{T+1|T}(n)
performs the best in this exercise,
which outperforms
\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\text{AR}}_{T+1|T}(n)

and
\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\text{FARM}}_{T+1|T}(n)
according
to both
\text{FE}^{\text{avg}}
and
\text{FE}^{\text{max}}
on average.
As noted in Appendix
E.2.2
, the forecast based on
\widehat{\bm{\chi}}^{\text{{unr}}}_{T+1|T}
shows instabilities and generally is outperformed by the one based on
\widehat{\bm{\chi}}^{\text{{res}}}_{T+1|T}
, but nonetheless performs reasonably well.
Given the high level of co-movements and persistence in the data, the good performance of FNETS is mainly attributed to the way we forecast the factor-driven component, which is based on the estimators derived under GFDM that fully exploit all the dynamic co-dependencies (see also the results obtained by
Barigozzi and Hallin, 2017
on a similar dataset).

6
Conclusions

We propose and study the asymptotic properties of FNETS, a network estimation and forecasting methodology
for high-dimensional time series under a dynamic factor-adjusted VAR model.
Our estimation strategy fully takes into account the latency of the VAR process of interest
via regularised YW estimation which, distinguished from the existing approaches, brings in methodological simplicity as well as theoretical benefits.
We investigate the theoretical properties of FNETS under general conditions permitting weak factors and heavier tails than sub-Gaussianity commonly imposed in the high-dimensional VAR literature, and provide new insights into the interplay between various quantities determining the sparsity of the networks underpinning VAR processes, factor strength and tail behaviour, on the estimation of those networks.
Simulation studies and an application to a panel of financial time series show that FNETS is particularly useful for network analysis as it is able to discover group structures as well as producing accurate forecasts for highly co-moving and persistent time series such as log-volatilities.
The R software
fnets
implementing FNETS is available from CRAN
(
Barigozzi et al., 2023
)
.

Appendix A
Estimation of VAR parameters and
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{G}}}
via Dantzig selector estimator

Recalling the notations in Section
3.3
, we consider the following constrained
\ell_{1}
-minimisation approach closely related to the Dantzig selector proposed for high-dimensional linear regression
(
Candes and Tao, 2007
)
, for the estimation of
\bm{\beta}=[\mathbf{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{A}_{d}]^{\top}
:

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\text{{DS}}}={\arg\min}_{\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{pd\times p}}\ |\mathbf{M}|_{1}\quad\text{subject to}\quad\left|\widehat{\mathbbm{G}}\mathbf{M}-\widehat{\mathbbm{g}}\right|_{\infty}\leq\lambda^{\text{{DS}}},

(A.1)

where
\lambda^{\text{{DS}}}>0
is a tuning parameter.

To investigate the theoretical properties of
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\text{{DS}}}
, we measure the (weak) sparsity of
\bm{\beta}
by
s_{0}(\varrho)=\sum_{j=1}^{p}s_{0,j}
with
s_{0,j}(\varrho)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}\sum_{k=1}^{p}|A_{\ell,jk}|^{\varrho}
for some
\varrho\in[0,1)
. In particular, when
\varrho=0
, they coincide with the sparsity measures defined in the main text, as
s_{0,j}=s_{0,j}(0)=|\bm{\beta}_{\cdot j}|_{0}
,
s_{0}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}s_{0,j}
and
s_{\text{in}}=\max_{1\leq j\leq p}s_{0,j}
.

Proposition A.1
.

Set
\lambda^{\text{{DS}}}\geq C_{\xi}(\|\bm{\beta}\|_{1}+1)(\vartheta_{n,p}\vee m^{-1}\vee p^{-1/2})
in (
A.1
). Then on
\mathcal{E}_{n,p}
defined in (
11
),
for any
\varrho\in[0,1)
,
conditional on
\mathcal{E}_{n,p}
defined in (
11
), we have

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\left|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\text{{DS}}}_{\cdot j}-\bm{\beta}_{\cdot j}\right|_{2}\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{s_{\text{in}}}\lambda^{\text{{DS}}}}{\pi m_{\xi}},\quad\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\left|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\text{{DS}}}_{\cdot j}-\bm{\beta}_{\cdot j}\right|_{1}\lesssim\frac{s_{\text{in}}\lambda^{\text{{DS}}}}{\pi m_{\xi}},

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\left|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\text{{DS}}}_{\cdot j}-\bm{\beta}_{\cdot j}\right|_{\infty}\lesssim\min\left(\|\mathbbm{G}^{-1}\|_{1}\lambda^{\text{{DS}}},\frac{\sqrt{s_{\text{in}}}\lambda^{\text{{DS}}}}{\pi m_{\xi}}\right)\quad\text{ and}

\displaystyle\left|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\text{{DS}}}-\bm{\beta}\right|_{1}\leq 6s_{0}(\varrho)\left(\|\mathbbm{G}^{-1}\|_{1}\lambda^{\text{{DS}}}\right)^{1-\varrho}.

Proposition
A.1
shows that
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\text{{DS}}}
achieves consistency when
\bm{\beta}
is only weakly sparse with
\varrho>0
, but the estimation error involves a multiplicative factor
\|\mathbbm{G}^{-1}\|_{1}
.
This is linked to the sparsity of the ACV matrices of
\bm{\xi}_{t}

which is related to, but is not fully captured by, the sparsity of
\bm{\beta}
.
For example,
Han et al., 2015
show that when
\mathbbm{G}
is strictly diagonally dominant
(
Horn and Johnson, 1985
, Definition 6.1.9)
with
\min_{1\leq i\leq p}\min_{1\leq k\leq d}(2|\gamma_{\xi,ii}(0)|-\sum_{\ell=-d+k}^{k-1}\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{p}|\gamma_{\xi,ii^{\prime}}(\ell)|)\geq\gamma_{\circ}>0
(where
\bm{\Gamma}_{\xi}(\ell)=[\gamma_{\xi,ii^{\prime}}(\ell)]_{i,i^{\prime}=1}^{p}
),
we have
\|\mathbbm{G}^{-1}\|_{1}\leq\gamma_{\circ}^{-1}
.

Corollary A.2
.

Suppose that the conditions of Proposition
A.1
are met.
If

\displaystyle\min_{(i,j)\in\text{supp}(\bm{\beta})}|\beta_{ij}|>2\mathfrak{t}

with
\mathfrak{t}=2\|\mathbbm{G}^{-1}\|_{1}\lambda^{\text{{DS}}}
,
we have
\text{{sign}}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\text{{DS}}}(\mathfrak{t}))=\text{{sign}}(\bm{\beta})
on
\mathcal{E}_{n,p}
.

Appendix B
Forecasting the factor-driven component under unrestricted GDFM

In this section, we present an alternative method for estimating the best linear predictor (
14
) of the common component without the restrictive assumption made in Section
4.1
.
The estimator, denoted by
\bm{\chi}^{\text{{unr}}}_{t+a|t}
, has been proposed by
Forni et al., 2017
, and we provide a new theoretical result that establishes the
\ell_{\infty}
-norm consistency of this estimator.

We first make a mild assumption that the filter
\mathcal{B}(L)
in (
1
) is rational:

Assumption B.1
.

Each filter
\mathcal{B}_{ij}(L)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}B_{\ell,ij}L^{\ell}
is a ratio of finite-order polynomials in
L
, i.e.
\mathcal{B}_{ij}(L)=(\mathcal{B}^{(2)}_{ij}(L))^{-1}\mathcal{B}^{(1)}_{ij}(L)
with

\mathcal{B}^{(k)}_{ij}(L)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{s^{(k)}}B^{(k)}_{\ell,ij}L^{\ell}
,
k=1,2
,
for all
1\leq i\leq p
and
1\leq j\leq q

with
s^{(1)},s^{(2)}\in\mathbb{N}
not dependent on
i
and
j
.
Moreover,

(i)

\max_{1\leq i\leq p}\max_{1\leq j\leq q}\max_{0\leq\ell\leq s^{(1)}}|B^{(1)}_{\ell,ij}|\leq B^{(1)}_{\chi}
for some constant
B^{(1)}_{\chi}>0
, and

(ii)

for all
1\leq i\leq p
and
1\leq j\leq q
, we have

\mathcal{B}^{(2)}_{ij}(z)\neq 0
for all
|z|\leq 1
.

Forni et al., 2015
establish that for generic values of the parameters
B^{(1)}_{\ell,ij}
and
B^{(2)}_{\ell,ij}
defined in Assumption
B.1
(i.e. outside a countable union of nowhere dense subsets),

\bm{\chi}_{t}
admits a blockwise VAR representation.
Supposing that
p=N(q+1)
with some integer
N
for convenience,
each
(q+1)
-dimensional block

\bm{\chi}_{t}^{(h)}=(\chi_{(q+1)(h-1)+i,t},\,1\leq i\leq q+1)^{\top}
,
1\leq h\leq N
,
admits a singular,
finite-order
VAR representation
with
\mathbf{u}_{t}
as the
q
-dimensional innovations.
We formally impose this genericity result as an assumption.

Assumption B.2

(Blockwise VAR representation)
.

(i)

\bm{\chi}_{t}
admits a blockwise VAR representation

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\chi}(L)\bm{\chi}_{t}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{A}_{\chi}^{(1)}(L)&\mathbf{O}&\ldots&\mathbf{O}\\
\mathbf{O}&\mathcal{A}_{\chi}^{(2)}(L)&\ldots&\mathbf{O}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\mathbf{O}&\mathbf{O}&\ldots&\mathcal{A}_{\chi}^{(N)}(L)\end{bmatrix}\;\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\chi}^{(1)}_{t}\\
\bm{\chi}^{(2)}_{t}\\
\vdots\\
\bm{\chi}^{(N)}_{t}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{R}^{(1)}\\
\mathbf{R}^{(2)}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{R}^{(N)}\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{u}_{t}=:\mathbf{R}\mathbf{u}_{t}

(B.1)

where, for each
h=1,\ldots,N
:

(a)

\mathcal{A}_{\chi}^{(h)}(L)=\mathbf{I}-\sum_{\ell=1}^{s_{h}}\mathbf{A}_{\chi,\ell}^{(h)}L^{\ell}

with
\mathbf{A}_{\chi,\ell}^{(h)}\in\mathbb{R}^{(q+1)\times(q+1)}

is of degree
s_{h}\leq qs^{(1)}+q^{2}s^{(2)}

and
\det(\mathcal{A}_{\chi}^{(h)}(z))\neq 0
for all
|z|\leq 1
.
Then, we define
\mathcal{A}_{\chi}(L)=\mathbf{I}-\sum_{\ell=1}^{s}\mathbf{A}_{\chi,\ell}L^{\ell}

with
s=\max_{1\leq h\leq N}s_{h}
where
\mathbf{A}^{(h)}_{\chi,\ell}=\mathbf{O}
for
\ell\geq s_{h}+1
.

(b)

\mathbf{R}^{(h)}\in\mathbb{R}^{(q+1)\times q}
is of rank
q

with
[\mathbf{R}^{(h)}]_{ij}=B^{(1)}_{0,ij}
.

(c)

The VAR representation in (
B.1
) is unique, i.e. if

\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\chi}^{(h)}(L)\bm{\chi}_{t}=\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}^{(h)}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{t}
,
the degree of
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\chi}^{(h)}(L)
does not exceed
s
and

\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{t}
is
q
-dimensional white noise, such that

\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\chi}^{(h)}(L)=\mathcal{A}_{\chi}^{(h)}(L)
,

\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}^{(h)}=\mathbf{R}^{(h)}\bm{\mathcal{O}}^{\top}
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{t}=\bm{\mathcal{O}}\mathbf{u}_{t}

for some orthogonal matrix
\bm{\mathcal{O}}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times q}
.

(ii)

For
h=1,\ldots,N
, define

\bm{\Gamma}^{(h)}_{\chi}(\ell)=\mathsf{E}(\bm{\chi}^{(h)}_{t-\ell}(\bm{\chi}^{(h)}_{t})^{\top})
,

\mathbf{B}^{(h)}_{\chi}=[(\bm{\Gamma}^{(h)}_{\chi}(\ell))^{\top},\,1\leq\ell\leq s_{h}]
and

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}^{(h)}_{\chi}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\Gamma}^{(h)}_{\chi}(0)&\bm{\Gamma}^{(h)}_{\chi}(-1)&\ldots&\bm{\Gamma}^{(h)}_{\chi}(-s_{h}+1)\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\bm{\Gamma}^{(h)}_{\chi}(s_{h}-1)&\bm{\Gamma}^{(h)}_{\chi}(s_{h}-2)&\ldots&\bm{\Gamma}^{(h)}_{\chi}(0)\end{bmatrix},

such that
\mathbf{A}^{(h)}_{\chi}=[\mathbf{A}^{(h)}_{\chi,\ell},\,1\leq\ell\leq s_{h}]=\mathbf{B}^{(h)}_{\chi}(\mathbf{C}^{(h)}_{\chi})^{-1}
.
Then, there exists a constant
c_{0}>0
such that

\min_{1\leq h\leq N}\det(\mathbf{C}^{(h)}_{\chi})>c_{0}
.

Non-singularity of
\mathbf{C}^{(h)}_{\chi}
is implied by
Assumption
B.2

(i)

but the condition
(ii)
is imposed
to ensure the boundedness of
\|\mathbf{A}^{(h)}_{\chi}\|
.
Let
\mathbf{Z}_{t}=\mathcal{A}_{\chi}(L)\mathbf{X}_{t}
such that under (
B.1
),

\displaystyle\mathbf{Z}_{t}=\mathbf{R}\mathbf{u}_{t}+\mathcal{A}_{\chi}(L)\bm{\xi}_{t}=:\mathbf{V}_{t}+\mathbf{W}_{t}.

(B.2)

Then,
\mathbf{Z}_{t}
admits a static factor representation with
\mathbf{u}_{t}
as (static) factors.
Let
\bm{\Gamma}_{z}
denote the covariance matrix of
\mathbf{Z}_{t}

and
\mu_{z,j}
its
j
-th largest eigenvalue,
and similarly define
\bm{\Gamma}_{v}
,
\mu_{v,j}
,
\bm{\Gamma}_{w}
and
\mu_{w,j}
.
As a consequence of Assumption
2.3
,
the eigenvalues of
\bm{\Gamma}_{w}
are bounded as below.

Proposition B.1
.

Under Assumptions
2.2
,
2.3
,
F.1
and
B.2
,
there exists some constant
B_{w}~>~0
such that
\mu_{w,1}\leq B_{w}
.

The following assumption,
imposed on the strength of the factors in (
B.2
)
similarly as in Assumptions
2.1
and
4.1

(ii)
,
enables asymptotic identification of
\mathbf{V}_{t}
and
\mathbf{W}_{t}
.
Due to the complexity associated with theoretical analysis under the model (
B.1
),
we consider the case of strong factors only.

Assumption B.3
.

There exist a positive integer
p_{0}\geq 1

and pairs of positive constants
(\alpha_{v,j},\beta_{v,j}),\,1\leq j\leq q
, such that
for all
p\geq p_{0}
,

\displaystyle\beta_{v,1}\geq\frac{\mu_{v,1}}{p}\geq\alpha_{v,1}>\beta_{v,2}\geq\frac{\mu_{v,2}}{p}\geq\ldots\geq\alpha_{v,q-1}>\beta_{v,q}\geq\frac{\mu_{v,q}}{p}\geq\alpha_{v,q}>0.

We propose to perform in-sample estimation and forecasting of the common component
by directly utilising the expression of
\bm{\chi}_{t+a|t}
in (
14
),
following the method proposed in
Forni et al., 2017

that makes use of the outcome of the dynamic PCA step outlined in Section
3.2
.

Step 1
:

Estimate
\mathcal{A}_{\chi}^{(h)}(L)

with
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\chi}^{(h)}(L)=\mathbf{I}-\sum_{\ell=1}^{s_{h}}\widehat{\mathbf{A}}^{(h)}_{\chi,\ell}L^{\ell}

via Yule-Walker estimator

\widehat{\mathbf{A}}^{(h)}_{\chi}=[\widehat{\mathbf{A}}^{(h)}_{\chi,\ell},\,1\leq\ell\leq s_{h}]=\widehat{\mathbf{B}}^{(h)}_{\chi}(\widehat{\mathbf{C}}^{(h)}_{\chi})^{-1}
,
where
\widehat{\mathbf{B}}^{(h)}_{\chi}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{C}}^{(h)}_{\chi}
are defined analogously as

\mathbf{B}^{(h)}_{\chi}
and
\mathbf{C}^{(h)}_{\chi}
,
with
\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\chi}(\ell)
defined in Section
3.2
replacing
\bm{\Gamma}_{\chi}(\ell)
.
Let
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\chi}(L)=\text{diag}(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\chi}^{(h)}(L),\,1\leq h\leq N)
.

Step 2
:

Obtain the filtered process

\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}=\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\chi}(L)\mathbf{X}_{t}
for
s+1\leq t\leq n
,
and let
\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{z}=(n-s)^{-1}\sum_{t=s+1}^{n}\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}^{\top}
,
and denote by
\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{z}=\sum_{j=1}^{p\wedge(n-s)}\widehat{\mu}_{z,j}\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{z,j}\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{z,j}^{\top}
its eigendecomposition.
Then, set

\widehat{\mathbf{R}}=[\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{z,j}\sqrt{\widehat{\mu}_{z,j}},\,1\leq j\leq q]=\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{z,1:q}\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{M}}}_{z,1:q}^{1/2}

and
\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{t}=\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{M}}}_{z,1:q}^{-1/2}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{z,1:q}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}

where
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{M}}}_{z,1:q}=\text{diag}(\widehat{\mu}_{z,j},\,1\leq j\leq q)

and
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{z,1:q}=[\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{z,j},\,1\leq j\leq q]
.

Step 3
:

Denoting by
\widehat{\mathbf{B}}_{\ell}
the coefficient matrix multiplied to
L^{\ell}

in expanding
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\chi}^{-1}(L)\widehat{\mathbf{R}}
,
with some truncation lag
K
, we estimate
\bm{\chi}_{t+a|t}
by

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\chi}}^{\text{{unr}}}_{t+a|t}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{K}\widehat{\mathbf{B}}_{\ell+a}\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{t-\ell}.

(B.3)

Remark B.1
.

(a)

In our numerical studies reported in Section
5.2
and Appendix
E.2
, we apply the Schwarz criterion
(
Lütkepohl, 2005
, Chapter 4)
to each block for selecting the order
s_{h}
of the blockwise VAR model (see Assumption
B.2
).
Also, we set the truncation lag at
K=20
in (
B.3
) and the number of cross-sectional permutations to be
30
by default.

(b)

The cross-sectional ordering of the panel has an impact on the selection of the diagonal blocks when estimating
\mathcal{A}_{\chi}(L)
. Each cross-sectional permutation of the panel leads to distinct estimators, all sharing the same asymptotic properties. Using a Rao-Blackwell-type argument,
Forni et al., 2017
advocates the aggregation of these estimators into a unique one by simple averaging (after obvious reordering of the cross-sections). Although averaging over all
p!
permutations is infeasible, as argued by
Forni et al., 2017
and empirically verified by
Forni et al., 2018
, a few of them are enough in practice to deliver stable averages.

(c)

When
p
is not an integer multiple of
(q+1)
,
we can consider
\lfloor p/(q+1)\rfloor-1
blocks of size
(q+1)

along with a block of the remaining
(q+1)+p-\lfloor p/(q+1)\rfloor(q+1)
variables.
All the theoretical arguments used in
Forni et al., 2017
and in this paper apply to any partition of the cross-section into blocks of size
(q+1)
or larger (but finite).

(d)

It is known that as the VAR order
s_{h}
increases,
the estimation of a singular VAR via Yule-Walker methods
might become unstable since it requires inverting
\widehat{\mathbf{C}}^{(h)}_{\chi}
,
a Toeplitz matrix of dimension
s_{h}(q+1)\times s_{h}(q+1)
.
To address potential issues arising from this,

Hörmann and Nisol, 2021
propose a regularised approach
aimed at stabilising the estimate of
\mathcal{A}_{\chi}^{(h)}(L)
.
Empirically, such an approach leads to better performance
and can be taken when
s_{h}
is large.

Let us denote the matrix collecting all the transition matrices involved
in the blockwise VAR model in (
B.1
) by

\mathbf{A}_{\chi}=[\mathbf{A}_{\chi,1},\ldots,\mathbf{A}_{\chi,s}]\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times ps}

and its estimated counterpart by
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{\chi}
.
Then, the estimators from the above procedure satisfy the following.

Proposition B.2
.

Suppose that the conditions in Theorem
3.1
are met,
including Assumption
2.1
which is satisfied with

\rho_{j}=1,\,1\leq j\leq q
.
Further let Assumptions
B.2
–
B.3
hold.

(i)

Denoting by
\bm{\varphi}_{i}
a vector of zeros
except for its
i
-th element being set to one, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\left\|\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{\chi}-\mathbf{A}_{\chi}\right\|_{F}

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\vartheta_{n,p}\vee\frac{1}{m}\vee\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\right)\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq i\leq p}\left|\bm{\varphi}_{i}^{\top}\left(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{\chi}-\mathbf{A}_{\chi}\right)\right|_{2}

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\vartheta_{n,p}\vee\frac{1}{m}\vee\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\right),

where
\vartheta_{n,p}
is defined in (
F.1
).

(ii)

For any given
t
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\widehat{\mathbf{R}}\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{t}-\mathbf{R}\mathbf{u}_{t}\right|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\vartheta_{n,p}\vee\frac{1}{m}\vee\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\right).

(iii)

For any fixed
a\geq 0
and
t
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\widehat{\bm{\chi}}^{\text{{unr}}}_{t+a|t}-{\bm{\chi}}_{t+a|t}\right|_{\infty}=O_{P}\left(KM_{\chi}^{K}\left(\vartheta_{n,p}\vee\frac{1}{m}\vee\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\right)+\log^{1/2}(p)K^{-\varsigma+1}\right),

where
M_{\chi}>0
is a constant.
Further, when
p=O(n^{\kappa})
for some
\kappa>0
,
we can select
K=K_{n}=\lfloor c\log(n)\rfloor
with a small enough
c>0

such that

\left|\widehat{\bm{\chi}}^{\text{{unr}}}_{t+a|t}-{\bm{\chi}}_{t+a|t}\right|_{\infty}=o_{P}(1)
.

Proposition
B.2

(iii)
,
combined with Proposition
4.2
,
concludes the analysis of the forecasting error
|\widehat{\bm{\chi}}^{\text{{unr}}}_{t+a|t}-\bm{\chi}_{t+a}|_{\infty}
.

Remark B.2
.

(a)

In Proposition
B.2
,
we assume a stronger factor structure
given by Assumption
2.1
with
\rho_{j}=1
for all
1\leq j\leq q
,
and Assumption
B.3
for ease of presentation;
as noted in Remark
3.1

(c)
,
weaker factor strength would imply worse estimation and forecasting performance.

(b)

Proposition
B.2

(i)
extends
Proposition 9 of
Forni et al., 2017
that considers the consistency of
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{\chi}

for a given single block.
The result in
(ii)
indicates that
we can consistently recover
\mathbf{V}_{t}=\mathbf{R}\mathbf{u}_{t}

and hence the static factor space.
Moreover, parts
(i)
and
(ii)
imply that we can consistently recover the space spanned by
\mathbf{u}_{t}
and their associated impulse response functions
{\mathcal{B}}(L)={\mathcal{A}}^{-1}_{\chi}(L)\mathbf{R}

for finitely many lags up to a linear transformation.
This is particularly useful in empirical macroeconomic literature where typically,

\mathbf{u}_{t}
carries a specific economic implication
(e.g. shocks related to monetary policy, fiscal policy,
demand, supply, technology and oil, to name a few).
Indeed, it is a common practice to use the above estimation method to
recover the space spanned by these shocks and then to identify their dynamic effect
\mathcal{B}(L)

by imposing
ad hoc
economic based restrictions;
see, e.g.
Stock and Watson, 2016
, for a review of this approach.

(c)

In Proposition
B.2

(iii)
,
the constant
M_{\chi}
is associated with the operator norm
of the transition matrix involved in the VAR(
1
) representation of (
B.1
)
and, provided that the maximum VAR order
s=\max_{1\leq h\leq N}s_{h}\geq 2
,
we have
M_{\chi}\geq 1
(see Appendix E of
Basu and Michailidis, 2015
).
The
O_{P}
bound in
(iii)

can be made to converge to zero as
n,p\to\infty
by selecting an appropriate truncation lag
K
,
but it is at a slower rate compared to the
O_{P}
bound derived for
\widehat{\bm{\chi}}^{\text{{res}}}_{t}

studied in Section
4.1

when comparable strong factors are assumed (
\rho_{j}=1,\,1\leq j\leq q
, in Assumption
2.1

and
\varrho_{j}=1,\,1\leq j\leq r
, in Assumption
4.1

(ii)
).
This shows the advantage of working under a more restrictive factor model
in Section
4.1
when it comes to forecasting.

Appendix C
Sparsity structure of
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{L}}}

Recall that
\bm{\Omega}=[\omega_{ii^{\prime}}]
and
\bm{\Delta}=[\delta_{ii^{\prime}}]
,
and let
\mathcal{A}(1)=[a_{ii^{\prime}}]
. Also, define
g_{i,i^{\prime}}=1
if
i=i^{\prime}
and
g_{i,i^{\prime}}=0
otherwise.
Recall that
a_{ii^{\prime}}=g_{i,i^{\prime}}-\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}A_{\ell,ii^{\prime}}
.
Then,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\omega_{ii^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{h=1}^{p}\sum_{k=1}^{p}a_{hi}\delta_{hk}a_{ki^{\prime}}=a_{ii}\sum_{k}\delta_{ik}a_{ki^{\prime}}+\sum_{h}\sum_{k}a_{hi}\delta_{hk}a_{ki^{\prime}}(1-g_{h,i})

\displaystyle=a_{ii}\sum_{\ell}\delta_{i\ell}a_{\ell i^{\prime}}+a_{i^{\prime}i^{\prime}}\sum_{\ell}a_{\ell i}\delta_{\ell i^{\prime}}(1-g_{\ell,i})+\sum_{h}\sum_{k}a_{hi}\delta_{hk}a_{ki^{\prime}}(1-g_{h,i})(1-g_{k,i^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=a_{ii}\delta_{ii^{\prime}}a_{i^{\prime}i^{\prime}}+a_{i^{\prime}i^{\prime}}\delta_{i^{\prime}i^{\prime}}a_{i^{\prime}i}+a_{ii}\delta_{ii}a_{ii^{\prime}}+a_{ii}\sum_{\ell}\delta_{i\ell}a_{\ell i^{\prime}}(1-g_{\ell,i})(1-g_{\ell,i^{\prime}})+

\displaystyle\qquad a_{i^{\prime}i^{\prime}}\sum_{\ell}a_{\ell i}\delta_{\ell i^{\prime}}(1-g_{\ell,i})(1-g_{\ell,i^{\prime}})+\sum_{\ell}a_{\ell i}\delta_{\ell\ell}a_{\ell i^{\prime}}(1-g_{\ell,i})(1-g_{\ell,i^{\prime}})+

\displaystyle\qquad\sum_{h}\sum_{k}a_{hi}\delta_{hk}a_{ki^{\prime}}(1-g_{h,i})(1-g_{k,i^{\prime}})(1-g_{h,k}).

(C.1)

We conclude that
\omega_{ii^{\prime}}=0
if none of the following holds:
(i) variables
i
and
i^{\prime}
are partially correlated, i.e.
\delta_{ii^{\prime}}\neq 0
(from the first term in (
C.1
));
(ii)
i
Granger causes
i^{\prime}
in the long run, i.e.
\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}A_{\ell,i^{\prime}i}\neq 0
(from the second term);
(iii)
i^{\prime}
Granger causes
i
in the long run, i.e.
\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}A_{\ell,ii^{\prime}}\neq 0
(from the third term);
(iv) there exists a variable
j\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\{i,i^{\prime}\}
such that

\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}A_{\ell,ji}\neq 0
and
\delta_{i^{\prime}j}\neq 0
, or

\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}A_{\ell,ji^{\prime}}\neq 0
and
\delta_{ij}\neq 0
, or

\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}A_{\ell,ji}\neq 0
and
\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}A_{\ell,ji^{\prime}}\neq 0
(from the fourth, the fifth and the sixth terms); or
(v) there exist a pair of variables
j,j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\{i,i^{\prime}\}
such that

\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}A_{\ell,ji}\neq 0
,

\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}A_{\ell,j^{\prime}i^{\prime}}\neq 0
and
\delta_{jj^{\prime}}\neq 0
(from the last term).
As such, the edge set
\mathcal{E}^{\text{{L}}}
is typically larger than
\mathcal{E}^{\text{{G}}}\cup\mathcal{E}^{\text{{C}}}
.

Appendix D
Data-driven choice of the thresholds

Motivated by
Liu et al., 2021
, we propose a method for data-driven selection of the threshold
\mathfrak{t}
,
which is applied to the estimators of
\bm{\beta}=[\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\,1\leq\ell\leq d]^{\top}
,
\bm{\Delta}
or
\bm{\Omega}
for estimating the edge sets of
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{G}}}
,
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{C}}}
or
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{L}}}
, respectively.

Let
\mathbf{B}=[b_{ij}]\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
denote a matrix for which a threshold is to be selected, i.e.
\mathbf{B}
may be either
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
,
\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}_{0}
(
\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}
with diagonals set to zero) or
\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}_{0}
(
\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}
with diagonals set to zero) obtained from Steps 2 and 3 of FNETS.
We work with
\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}_{0}
and
\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}_{0}
since we do not threshold the diagonal entries of
\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}
.
As such estimators have been shown to achieve consistency in
\ell_{\infty}
-norm (Propositions
3.3
and
3.5
), we expect there exists a large gap between the entries of
\mathbf{B}
corresponding to true positives and false positives.
Further, it is expected that the number of edges reduces at a faster rate when increasing the threshold from
0
towards this (unknown) gap, compared to when increasing the threshold from the gap to
|\mathbf{B}|_{\infty}
.
Therefore, we propose to identify this gap by casting the problem as that of locating a single change point in the trend of the ratio of edges to non-edges,

\displaystyle\text{Ratio}_{k}=\frac{|\mathbf{B}(\mathfrak{t}_{k})|_{0}}{\max(N-|\mathbf{B}(\mathfrak{t}_{k})|_{0},1)},\quad k=1,\dots,M.

Here,
\mathbf{B}(\mathfrak{t})=[b_{ij}\cdot\mathbb{I}_{\{|b_{ij}|>\mathfrak{t}\}}]
,

|\mathbf{B}(\mathfrak{t})|_{0}=\sum_{i=1}^{m_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{2}}\mathbb{I}_{\{|b_{ij}|>\mathfrak{t}\}}

and
\{\mathfrak{t}_{k},\,1\leq k\leq M:\,0=\mathfrak{t}_{1}<\mathfrak{t}_{2}<\dots<\mathfrak{t}_{M}=|\mathbf{B}|_{\infty}\}
denotes a sequence of candidate threshold values.
We recommend using an exponentially growing sequence for
\{\mathfrak{t}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{M}
since the size of the false positive entries tends to be very small.
The quantity
N
in the denominator of Ratio
k
is set as
N=p^{2}d
when
\mathbf{B}=\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
, and
N=p(p-1)
when
\mathbf{B}=\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}_{0}
or
\mathbf{B}=\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}_{0}
.
Then, from the difference quotient

\displaystyle\text{Diff}_{k}=\frac{\text{Ratio}_{k}-\text{Ratio}_{k-1}}{\mathfrak{t}_{k}-\mathfrak{t}_{k-1}},\quad k=2,\ldots,M,

we compute the cumulative sum (CUSUM) statistic

\displaystyle\text{CUSUM}_{k}=\sqrt{\frac{k(M-k)}{M}}\left|\frac{1}{k}\sum_{l=2}^{k}\text{Diff}_{l}-\frac{1}{M-k}\sum_{l=k+1}^{M}\text{Diff}_{l}\right|,\quad k=2,\ldots,M-1,

and select
\mathfrak{t}=\mathfrak{t}_{k^{*}}
with
k^{*}={\arg\max}_{2\leq k\leq M-1}\text{CUSUM}_{k}
.

We investigate the performance of the thus-chosen thresholds on simulated datasets in Appendix
E
, see also
Owens et al., 2023
.

Appendix E
Simulation studies

E.1
Set-up

We apply FNETS to datasets simulated under a variety of settings, from Gaussian innovations
\mathbf{u}_{t}
and
\bm{\varepsilon}_{t}
with
(E1)

\bm{\Delta}=\mathbf{I}
and
(E2)

\bm{\Delta}\neq\mathbf{I}
, to
(E3)
heavy-tailed (
t_{5}
) innovations with
\bm{\Delta}=\mathbf{I}
,
and when
\bm{\chi}_{t}
is generated from
(C1)
fully dynamic or
(C2)
static factor models.
In addition, we consider the ‘oracle’ setting
(C0)

\bm{\chi}_{t}=\mathbf{0}
where, in the absence of the factor-driven component, the results obtained can serve as a benchmark.
We also include the factor-adjusted regression method of
Fan et al., 2021
which is referred to as FARM, and present the performance of their estimator
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\text{FARM}}
of VAR parameters and forecasts (see Appendix
E.1
for full descriptions).
For each setting,
100
realisations are generated.

The idiosyncratic component is generated as a VAR(
1
) process.
Let
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{G}}}
denote a directed Erdős-Rényi random graph
on
\mathcal{V}=\{1,\ldots,p\}
with the link probability
1/p
.
Then, the entries of
\mathbf{A}_{1}
are
A_{1,ii^{\prime}}=0.275
when
(i,i^{\prime})\in\mathcal{E}^{\text{{G}}}

and
A_{1,ii^{\prime}}=0
otherwise.
The innovations are generated according to the following three scenarios:

(E1)

Gaussian with
\bm{\Gamma}=\mathbf{I}
.

(E2)

Gaussian with
\bm{\Gamma}=\bm{\Delta}^{-1}
, where

\delta_{ii}=1.5
for
1\leq i\leq p
,
\delta_{ii^{\prime}}=-1/\sqrt{d_{i}d_{i^{\prime}}}
if
(i,i^{\prime})\in\mathcal{E}^{\text{{C}}}

and
\delta_{ii^{\prime}}=0
otherwise.
Here,
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{C}}}
is an undirected Erdős-Rényi random graph on
\mathcal{V}

with the link probability
1/p
, and
d_{i}
denotes the degree of the
i
-th node in
\mathcal{E}^{\text{{C}}}
.
This model is taken from
Barigozzi and Brownlees, 2019
.

(E3)

Heavy-tailed with

\sqrt{5/3}\cdot\varepsilon_{it}\sim_{\text{iid}}t_{5}
(such that
\mathsf{Var}(\varepsilon_{it})=1
)
and
\bm{\Gamma}=\mathbf{I}
.

We consider two models for the generation of factor-driven common component:

(C1)

Taken from
Forni et al., 2017
,

\chi_{it}
is generated as sum of
q
AR processes

\chi_{it}=\sum_{j=1}^{q}a_{ij}(1-\alpha_{ij}L)^{-1}u_{jt}
,
where
a_{ij}\sim_{\text{iid}}\mathcal{U}[-1,1]
and
\alpha_{ij}\sim_{\text{iid}}\mathcal{U}[-0.8,0.8]

with
\mathcal{U}[a,b]
denoting a uniform distribution.
This model does not admit a static factor model representation,
and we consider
q=2
.

(C2)

\chi_{it}
admits a static factor model representation as

\chi_{it}=a_{i}\sum_{\ell=1}^{2}\bm{\lambda}_{i\ell}^{\top}\mathbf{f}_{t-\ell+1}

with
\mathbf{f}_{t}=\mathbf{D}\mathbf{f}_{t-1}+\mathbf{u}_{t}
;
here,
\mathbf{F}_{t}=(\mathbf{f}_{t}^{\top},\mathbf{f}_{t-1}^{\top})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}

denotes the static factor with
r=2q
,

\mathbf{f}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
the dynamic factor and
and
\mathbf{u}_{t}=(u_{1t},\ldots,u_{qt})^{\top}
the common shocks.
The entries of the loadings
\bm{\lambda}_{i\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}

are generated i.i.d. from
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
,
and
\mathbf{D}=0.7\cdot\mathbf{D}_{0}/\Lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{D}_{0})

where the off-diagonal entries of
\mathbf{D}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times q}

are generated i.i.d. from
\mathcal{U}[0,0.3]

and its diagonal entries from
\mathcal{U}[0.5,0.8]
.
The multiplicative factor
a_{i}
is chosen for each realisation to keep
sample estimate of
\mathsf{Var}(\chi_{it})/\mathsf{Var}(\xi_{it})
at one.
We fix
q=2
(such that
r=4
).

Additionally, we consider the following ‘oracle’ setting:

(C0)

\bm{\chi}_{t}=0
,
i.e. the idiosyncratic VAR process is directly observed as
\mathbf{X}=\bm{\xi}_{t}
.

We vary
(n,p)\in\{(100,50),(100,100),(200,50),(200,100),(500,100),(500,200)\}
.
According to the distribution of
\bm{\varepsilon}_{t}
, we also vary the distribution of
\mathbf{u}_{t}
;
under
(E1)
or
(E2)
,
u_{jt}\sim_{\text{iid}}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
while
under
(E3)
,
\sqrt{5/3}\cdot u_{jt}\sim_{\text{iid}}t_{5}
.

E.2
Results

E.2.1
Network estimation

Throughout, we refer to the estimator in (
8
) by
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\text{{las}}}
, and report the results from both
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\text{{las}}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\text{{DS}}}
obtained as in (
A.1
).
Also,
\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}^{\text{{las}}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}^{\text{{DS}}}
denote the estimators of
\bm{\Omega}
obtained with
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\text{{las}}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\text{{DS}}}
, respectively see Section
3.4
.
For comparison, we consider the FARM methodology of
Fan et al., 2021
:
we implement their factor-adjustment step under a static factor model with the information criterion-based factor number estimator of
Alessi et al., 2010
and, to the residuals from removing factors, we apply the Lasso to estimate the VAR parameters using the R package
glmnet

(
Friedman et al., 2010
)
.
The resultant estimator is referred to as
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\text{FARM}}
.

In Tables
E.1
–
E.3
, we report the estimation errors
of
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\text{{las}}}
,
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\text{{DS}}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\text{FARM}}
in estimating
\bm{\beta}
,
and in Tables
E.2
–
E.4
,
those of
\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}^{\text{{las}}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\Omega}}^{\text{{DS}}}

in estimating
\bm{\Omega}
averaged over
100
realisations, and the corresponding standard errors.
We also report the results from estimating
\bm{\Delta}
by
\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}^{\text{{las}}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}^{\text{{DS}}}
in Table
E.5
.

With a matrix
\bm{\gamma}
as an estimand
we measure the estimation error of its estimator
\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}

using the (scaled) matrix norms:

\displaystyle L_{F}=\frac{\|\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}-\bm{\gamma}\|_{F}}{\|\bm{\gamma}\|_{F}}\quad\text{and}\quad L_{2}=\frac{\|\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}-\bm{\gamma}\|}{\|\bm{\gamma}\|}.

To assess the performance of
\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}

in recovering of the support of
\bm{\gamma}=[\gamma_{ii^{\prime}}]
,
i.e.
\{(i,i^{\prime}):\,\gamma_{ii^{\prime}}\neq 0\}
,
we generate receiver operating characteristic (ROC) curves of
true positive rate (TPR) against false positive rate (FPR),
averaged over
100
realisations for each setting:

\displaystyle\text{TPR}=\frac{|\{(i,i^{\prime}):\,\widehat{\gamma}_{ii^{\prime}}\neq 0\text{ and }\gamma_{ii^{\prime}}\neq 0\}|}{|\{(i,i^{\prime}):\,\gamma_{ii^{\prime}}\neq 0\}|}\quad\text{and}\quad\text{FPR}=\frac{|\{(i,i^{\prime}):\,\widehat{\gamma}_{ii^{\prime}}\neq 0\text{ and }\gamma_{ii^{\prime}}=0\}|}{|\{(i,i^{\prime}):\,\gamma_{ii^{\prime}}=0\}|},

(E.1)

see Figures
E.2
–
E.3
.
We additionally report the results of the TPR value when FPR is set at
0.05
, with and without thresholding the estimators as described in Section
5.1
,
in Tables
E.1
–
E.5
.

Overall, we observe that with increasing
n
, the performance of all estimators improve according to all metrics regardless of the data generating processes while increasing
p
has an adverse effect.
Generally, whether the factor-driven component admits a static representation as in
(C2)
or not as in
(C1)
, FNETS produces estimators of
\bm{\beta}
that perform as well as those applied under the oracle setting of
(C0)
with
\bm{\chi}_{t}=\mathbf{0}
.
Also
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
outperforms
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}^{\text{FARM}}
in all settings, particularly in estimating the support of
\bm{\beta}
(i.e. the edge set of
\mathcal{N}^{\text{{G}}}
) even without any thresholding in all scenarios.
FARM tends to produce highly sparse estimators with low TPR, see Figure
E.1
(averaged ROC curves are not necessarily monotonic as it contains pointwise average TPR at given FPR).
This is attributed to the accumulation of errors from estimating
\bm{\xi}_{t},\,1\leq t\leq n
, which possibly leads to low signal-to-noise ratio when estimating the VAR parameters via Lasso.
This difference vanishes as both
n
and
p
increase.
As noted in Introduction, FNETS and FARM have distinctive objectives, and FNETS is specifically proposed for network estimation under the proposed factor-adjusted VAR model.
When
\bm{\Delta}=\mathbf{I}
(as in
(E1)
and
(E3)
), FNETS estimates
\bm{\Omega}
with accuracy regardless of the tail behaviour of
\bm{\varepsilon}_{t}
and
\mathbf{u}_{t}
.
When
\bm{\Delta}\neq\mathbf{I}
, it tends to incur larger errors in estimating
\bm{\Omega}
compared to when
\bm{\Delta}=\mathbf{I}
, which is more noticeable in terms of support recovery (see Figure
E.4
).
This possibly stems from the performance of
\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}
(see Table
E.5
) rather than
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
, which becomes worse when
\bm{\Delta}\neq\mathbf{I}
.
Since the support of
\bm{\Omega}
depends on those of
\bm{\beta}
and
\bm{\Delta}
in a complex way (see Proposition
3.5

(ii)
and Appendix
C
), its estimation tends to be more challenging.

.

E.2.2
Forecasting

We assess the performance of the forecasting methodology of FNETS which estimates the best linear predictors
\bm{\chi}_{n+1|n}
by one of the two estimators,
\widehat{\bm{\chi}}_{n+1|n}^{\text{{res}}}
(Section
4.1
) and
\widehat{\bm{\chi}}_{n+1|n}^{\text{{unr}}}
(Appendix
B
),

\bm{\xi}_{n+1|n}
by
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}^{\text{{las}}}_{n+1|n}
and
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}^{\text{{DS}}}_{n+1|n}
(denoting the estimators of
\bm{\xi}_{n+1|n}
with Lasso and DS estimators of
\bm{\beta}
, respectively) and finally,
\mathbf{X}_{n+1|n}=\bm{\chi}_{n+1|n}+\bm{\xi}_{n+1|n}
by their combinations.
The estimator
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}_{n+1|n}
depends on the choice of the in-sample estimator of
\bm{\chi}_{t}
(which automatically yields the in-sample estimator of
\bm{\xi}_{t}
)
but we suppress this dependence in the notations.

Fan et al., 2021
propose a forecasting methodology based on VAR modelling of the estimated factors, the results from which we report alongside those from FNETS under the heading FARM.

In Tables
E.7
(under
(E1)
) and
E.11
(under
(E2)
–
(E3)
), we report the estimation errors of a given forecast, say
\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}_{n+1|n}
,
in estimating
\bm{\gamma}_{n+1|n}
measured as

\displaystyle\frac{|\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}_{n+1|n}-\bm{\gamma}_{n+1|n}|_{2}^{2}}{|\bm{\gamma}_{n+1|n}|_{2}^{2}}

(E.2)

and additionally, report the in-sample estimation errors of

\widehat{\bm{\chi}}_{t}=\widehat{\bm{\chi}}_{t}^{\text{{res}}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\chi}}_{t}=\widehat{\bm{\chi}}_{t}^{\text{{unr}}}

measured as

\sum_{t}|\widehat{\bm{\chi}}_{t}-\bm{\chi}_{t}|_{2}^{2}/(\sum_{t}|\bm{\chi}_{t}|_{2}^{2})
.
Tables
E.8
(under
(E1)
) and Tables
E.12
(under
(E2)
–
(E3)
) summarise the forecasting errors measured by

\displaystyle\frac{|\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}_{n+1|n}-\bm{\gamma}_{n+1|n}|_{\infty}}{|\bm{\gamma}_{n+1|n}|_{\infty}},

(E.3)

which relates to the norm chosen for theoretical analysis
in Propositions
4.1
and
B.2
and Proposition
4.3
.
We also report the forecasting errors measured as

\displaystyle\frac{|\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}_{n+1|n}-\bm{\gamma}_{n+1}|_{2}^{2}}{|\bm{\gamma}_{n+1}|_{2}},\quad\text{and}

(E.4)

\displaystyle\frac{|\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}_{n+1|n}-\bm{\gamma}_{n+1}|_{\infty}^{2}}{|\bm{\gamma}_{n+1}|_{\infty}},

(E.5)

see Tables
E.9
,
E.10
(under
(E1)
),
E.13
and
E.14
(under
(E2)
–
(E3)
).
Additionally, Table
E.6
contains results in the above error measures obtained from the benchmark case when
\mathbf{X}_{t}=\bm{\xi}_{t}
under
(C0)
when
\bm{\xi}_{t}
is generated according to
(E1)
.

For FNETS, the forecasting performance improves as
n
increases regardless of the error measures.
The estimation error for
\bm{\chi}_{n+1|n}
decreases with
p

while it increases for
\bm{\xi}_{n+1|n}
,
which is due to that the factor-adjustment step enjoys the blessing of dimensionality
while VAR estimation tends to suffer from the increase of the dimensionality.
This observation is consistent with Propositions
4.1
and
4.3
, and the former tends to offset latter in the estimation error of
\mathbf{X}_{n+1|n}
.
The forecasting method based on the unrestricted GDFM (Appendix
B
) exhibits some numerical instabilities due to the instability of the singular VAR equation system adopted for this purpose (see Remark
B.1

(d)
)
which, in turn, may be attributed to the possible
over-specification of the VAR order
(
Hörmann and Nisol, 2021
)
.
As such, the performance of
\widehat{\bm{\chi}}_{n+1|n}^{\text{{res}}}
is generally superior even when
\bm{\chi}_{t}
does not admit a static representation (under
(C1)
),
and the gap between the two estimators gets wider when a static representation exists (under
(C2)
) as
n
and
p
increase.
In general, we do not observe any systematic effect of the innovation distribution on the forecasting performance.

FARM performs reasonably well when
n\geq 200
. In particular, when
\bm{\chi}_{t}
is generated under
(C2)
, the static factor model offers a valid alternative to GDFM such that FARM marginally outperforms FNETS in in-sample estimation and estimating
\bm{\chi}_{n+1|n}
.
However, the performance in estimating the VAR parameters carries forward to producing the forecast of
\bm{\xi}_{n+1}
, which sometimes result in slightly worse forecasts of
\mathbf{X}_{n+1}
.

Under
(C2)
, occasionally the best linear predictor
\bm{\chi}_{n+1|n}

has all its elements close to zero and
the small value of
|\bm{\chi}_{n+1|n}|_{2}
inflates
the relative estimation error measured as in (
E.2
);
this phenomenon is not observed
from the forecasting errors measured with (
E.4
).
